% The drop zeta paper beside the series (C86, C89, 24 Nov 2026), with the best of the Order (B120, the paper
% beside the series of 18-19 Nov 2026, Draft (46)) and the floors (C100, Draft (9)) merged into it (C116, 25 Nov
% 2026; the user: "best" is the final power of "floor"; the best and the floors come early: they set the bounds).
% Research level, self-contained; papers 1-3 cited. Lean: Lean/accelerated_collatz/AcceleratedCollatz/Zeta/
% (Floor/, Best/, namespace AcceleratedCollatz.Best); scripts and data of the best: Lean/accelerated_collatz/zeta/.
% Sources: Project_kb/Collatz_zeta.md (C79), _keep/czeta/ (local), _keep/tree_01/ (local). \fillin marks values still to come.
% Figures: media/octave_tikz.py and media/floors_tikz.py write between their BEGIN/END lines below (do not edit
% there); media/turn_tikz.py and media/tree_tikz.py make \dropturnfig and \droptreefig.
% Drafts: zeta (1) to (9), 24-25 Nov 2026 ((8): Section Compared with zeta; (9): Proposition prop:primes,
% the primes F = 3/4 and its powers, C113.1); (10): the best and the floors merged in (C116, 25 Nov 2026);
% (11): Proposition prop:tilt (C117), the staircase as a corollary in floor terms (C119).
\documentclass[10pt,a4paper,titlepage]{article}
\usepackage[utf8]{inputenc}
\usepackage[T1]{fontenc}
\usepackage{amsmath,amssymb,amsthm}
\usepackage[margin=1.25in]{geometry}
\usepackage{hyperref}
\hypersetup{colorlinks, allcolors=blue!50!black}
% Index: the entry of a key term links to the passage that introduces the term
\makeatletter
\newcommand{\termhere}[1]{\leavevmode
  \Hy@raisedlink{\hyper@anchorstart{term:#1}\hyper@anchorend}}
\newcommand{\boxhere}[1]{\leavevmode
  \Hy@raisedlink{\hyper@anchorstart{box:#1}\hyper@anchorend}}
\makeatother
\newcommand{\termlink}[2]{\hyperlink{term:#1}{#2}}
\newcommand{\oeisidx}[1]{\index{OEIS!#1@\protect\hyperlink{cite.OEIS_#1}{#1}}}
% A key term where it is introduced: italic, with a link to its entry in the box
% of its chapter (\boxhere)
% a key term on a light blue background (user, 20 Nov 2026)
\newcommand{\linkbg}[1]{{\setlength{\fboxsep}{1pt}\colorbox{blue!8}{\strut\emph{#1}}}}
\newcommand{\keyterm}[2]{\hyperlink{box:#1}{\linkbg{#2}}}
% A symbol that is a key term, where its meaning is given: the same link, no italics
\newcommand{\keysym}[2]{\hyperlink{box:#1}{#2}}
% A term used before its chapter (the Introduction): italic, linking to where it is introduced
\newcommand{\fwdterm}[2]{\hyperlink{term:#1}{\linkbg{#2}}}
\newcommand{\fwdsym}[2]{\hyperlink{term:#1}{#2}}
\usepackage{booktabs}
\usepackage{array}
\usepackage{longtable}
\usepackage{colortbl} % thin grey rules in the key-terms boxes
\usepackage{xcolor}
\usepackage{tikz}
\usepackage{etoolbox}
\usepackage[section]{placeins}
\IfFileExists{imakeidx.sty}
  {\usepackage{imakeidx}\makeindex[intoc]}
  {\usepackage{makeidx}\makeindex}
% keep an index item with its subitems (no column break between them)
\makeatletter\renewcommand\subitem{\par\nobreak\hangindent 40\p@ \hspace*{20\p@}}\makeatother

% Lean declaration
\newcommand{\lean}[1]{\textsf{#1}}
% Skeleton only: the plan of a proof, and a value still to fill in
\newcommand{\plan}[1]{\par\noindent{\small\color{black!60}\emph{Proof plan.} #1}\par}
\newcommand{\fillin}[1]{{\color{red!60!black}[#1]}}
\definecolor{arcblue}{HTML}{2062B4}
\definecolor{floorred}{HTML}{CC0000}
\definecolor{linedot}{HTML}{5A7F99}
\definecolor{stairamber}{HTML}{E69500}
\newcommand{\Z}{\mathbb{Z}}
\newcommand{\Q}{\mathbb{Q}}
\newcommand{\C}{\mathbb{C}}

\newcommand{\Lad}{\operatorname{Lad}}
\newcommand{\Drop}{\operatorname{Drop}}
\DeclareMathOperator{\Rea}{Re}
\DeclareMathOperator{\Ima}{Im}

% Layout: paper (default) or screen: pdflatex "\def\screenversion{}\input{file}" (as papers 1 and 2)
\newif\ifpaper
\papertrue
\ifdefined\screenversion \paperfalse \fi
% Paragraphs are never split across pages (paper version), so a chapter's first lines stay with its heading
\raggedbottom
\ifpaper
\interlinepenalty=10000
\postdisplaypenalty=10000
\fi
\ExplSyntaxOn
\seq_new:N \l__bw_parts_seq
\bool_new:N \l__bw_first_bool
\cs_new_protected:Npn \__bw_part:n #1
  {
    \fcolorbox{black!50}{black!5}
      {
        \bool_set_true:N \l__bw_first_bool
        \tl_map_inline:nn {#1}
          {
            \bool_if:NF \l__bw_first_bool { \textcolor{black!30}{\vrule width 0.3pt} }
            \bool_set_false:N \l__bw_first_bool
            \makebox[0.62em]{\rule[-0.4ex]{0pt}{2.2ex}\ttfamily ##1}
          }
      }
  }
% binary word: digits in cells, parts split at "-"
\NewDocumentCommand \bw { m }
  {
    \text
      {
        \group_begin:
        \setlength{\fboxsep}{0pt}
        \setlength{\fboxrule}{0.4pt}
        \seq_set_split:Nnn \l__bw_parts_seq { - } {#1}
        \seq_map_indexed_inline:Nn \l__bw_parts_seq
          {
            \int_compare:nNnF {##1} = {1} { \hspace{0.1em}\texttt{-}\hspace{0.1em} }
            \__bw_part:n {##2}
          }
        \group_end:
      }
  }
\ExplSyntaxOff
% Words without frames: pdflatex "\def\plainwords{}\input{file}"
\ifdefined\plainwords \RenewDocumentCommand \bw { m } {\text{\texttt{#1}}} \fi

% Glossary box at the end of each chapter
\newsavebox{\wordboxbin}
\newenvironment{wordbox}
  {\par\ifpaper\vspace*{\fill}\vspace{\bigskipamount}\else\medskip\fi
   \noindent\begin{lrbox}{\wordboxbin}%
   \begin{minipage}{\dimexpr\linewidth-6pt-2\fboxrule\relax}\small\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.2}\setlength{\arrayrulewidth}{0.25pt}\arrayrulecolor{black!35}%
   \begin{tabular}{@{}>{\bfseries\boldmath\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.24\linewidth}|p{\dimexpr0.76\linewidth-2\tabcolsep-\arrayrulewidth\relax}@{}}}
  {\end{tabular}\end{minipage}\end{lrbox}%
   \begingroup\setlength{\fboxsep}{0pt}%
   \fcolorbox{black!40}{black!5}{\begin{minipage}{\dimexpr\linewidth-2\fboxrule\relax}%
     {\setlength{\fboxsep}{3pt}\colorbox{black!18}{\makebox[\dimexpr\linewidth-6pt\relax][l]%
       {\small\bfseries\strut Key terms introduced in Section~\thesection}}}\par\nointerlineskip
     {\color{black!40}\rule{\linewidth}{0.4pt}}\par\nointerlineskip
     \vspace{3pt}\noindent\hspace*{3pt}\usebox{\wordboxbin}\par\vspace{3pt}%
   \end{minipage}}\endgroup\par\ifpaper\else\medskip\fi}

% A figure or table set where it stands, on one page with what follows it
\makeatletter
\newenvironment{herefloat}[1]
  {\par\addvspace{\intextsep}\noindent\begin{minipage}{\linewidth}%
   \def\@captype{#1}\centering}
  {\end{minipage}\par\nopagebreak\vspace{\intextsep}}
\makeatother

\ifpaper\pretocmd{\section}{\clearpage}{}{}\fi

\title{\textbf{Collatz, but stop at the drop}\\[0.5ex]
  \Large The drop zeta function}
\author{\textbf{Ruud H.G. van Tol}}
\date{\begin{tikzpicture}\dropturnfig\end{tikzpicture}\\[5ex]
  \textbf{Draft (11)}\\[8ex]
  {\small\copyright\ 2026 Ruud H.G. van Tol. Licensed under \href{https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/}{CC BY 4.0}.}
}

\begin{document}
\begingroup\hypersetup{pageanchor=false}\maketitle\endgroup

\begin{center}\textbf{Abstract}\end{center}
\begin{quotation}\small\noindent
Under the map $T(x) = x/2$ for even $x$ and $(3x+1)/2$ for odd $x$, a drop word of Order $n$ is the
parity word of the first $L = p_2(n)$ steps of the starts that first fall below themselves after
$n$ odd steps, $p_2(n)$ the bit length of $3^n$. Each word is a residue class modulo $2^L$ on which
$T^L$ is affine with slope $\theta_n = 3^n/2^L$, the floor of Order $n$, so the drop ratios
$T^L(x)/x$ of an Order lie between two bounds. From below, the floor: the floors lie in the octave
$[1/2, 1)$, and neighbouring floors of the Orders $0$ to $N$ differ by at most three ratios $3^k/2^l$
or $2^l/3^k$, the intervals of Pythagorean tuning. From above, the best of the Order, the class id with
the highest drop ratio: it is the one whose cycle point $x^\ast = \rho/(2^L - 3^n)$ lies closest to
it, it lies below $n/2$ times the smallest start, the search for it splits exactly into a start side
and a drop side that meet in one congruence modulo $3^w$, and the bests of Orders $1$ to $378$ are
computed, each with a certificate. With the floors as its primes, all powers of $F = 3/4$, we study
\[
Z(s) = \frac{1}{1 - P(s)}, \qquad P(s) = \sum_{\text{drop words}} 2^{-Ls}\,3^{-n(1-s)},
\]
the dynamical zeta function of the drop map on the $2$-adic integers. $P$ is entire and $Z$ is
meromorphic without zeros; its pole at $s = 1$ is Terras's theorem that almost every start drops. In
every horizontal band of height $2\pi/\ln 2$ there is exactly one pole, none on or to the right of
$\Rea s = 1$ but $s = 1$; the poles lie on one closed curve, sampled by the rotation by $\log_2 3$, and
come as close to $\Rea s = 1$ as the square of the distance of $k\log_2 3$ to the nearest integer. An
explicit formula, as Riemann's for the primes, writes the renewal count of the drop map as a parabola
plus one harmonic per pole. An identity gives the number of drop words of each Order (OEIS A100982)
from binomial coefficients alone, counted by the Orders whose floors lie higher. The identities, the
three gaps and the statements on the best are formalized in Lean~4 with Mathlib, without
\texttt{sorry}; the table of bests is script data.

\medskip\noindent\textbf{MSC2020:} 11B83 (primary); 11M41, 37C30, 11J71, 05A15, 90C32, 68V15.\\
\textbf{Keywords:} Collatz problem, $3x+1$ problem, parity vectors, dynamical zeta function,
three-distance theorem, Pythagorean tuning, fractional programming, Dinkelbach method, cycle lemma, Lean~4.
\end{quotation}

\clearpage
\section*{Main results}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Main results}

Drop words, their Order $n$ and length $L = p_2(n)$, class ids, the constant $\rho$ and the floors are
defined in Section~\ref{sec:words}. Write $N(n)$ for the number of drop words of Order $n$ (OEIS
A100982\oeisidx{A100982}~\cite{OEIS_A100982}), $m_n = N(n)/2^{p_2(n)}$ for the mass of Order $n$,
$\theta_n = 3^n/2^{p_2(n)}$ for its floor, $e_n = p_2(n) - n\alpha = -\log_2\theta_n$, and
$\alpha = \log_2 3$; so $P(s) = \sum_{n \ge 0} m_n \theta_n^{\,s-1}$.

\subsection*{The floors: the lower bound}

\begin{mainthm}[The three gaps; Section~\ref{sec:gaps}]
For every $N \ge 1$, the floors $\theta_0, \dots, \theta_N$, put on the octave as a circle ($1$ and $1/2$
the same point), cut it into arcs whose end ratios take at most three values: $2\theta_a$ and $1/\theta_b$,
$a$ and $b$ the last lower and upper record Orders up to $N$, and their product. The product is missing
exactly when $N + 1$ is a record Order.
\end{mainthm}

\subsection*{The best: the upper bound}

\begin{mainthm}[The cycle point; Section~\ref{sec:cycle}]
For a class id $c$ of Order $n$, with $x^\ast = \rho/(2^L - 3^n)$,
\[
1 - \frac{T^L(c)}{c} = \Bigl(1 - \frac{3^n}{2^L}\Bigr)\Bigl(1 - \frac{x^\ast}{c}\Bigr),
\]
so the best of the Order is the class id with the largest $\theta = x^\ast/c$; $c$ drops exactly when
$c > x^\ast$, and $x^\ast$ is the strict minimum of its own cycle.
\end{mainthm}

\begin{mainthm}[The window; Section~\ref{sec:search}]
If a class id of Order $n$ has $T^L(c)/c > g/h > 3^n/2^L$, then $c\,(2^L g - 3^n h) < R(n)\,h$, where
$R(n) = \sum_{q=1}^{n} 3^{n-q} 2^{p_2(q-1)-1}$ is the largest $\rho$ of the Order. Seeded with any
class id of Order $n$, a scan over the starts in increasing order is complete once it passes the
window of the best found; so is a best-first search over the word tree. The best $c^\ast$ lies
below $n\,s_0/2$ for $n \ge 3$, $s_0$ the smallest start of the Order (Corollary~\ref{cor:bestrange}).
\end{mainthm}

\begin{mainthm}[The meeting split; Section~\ref{sec:dinkelbach}]
Cut a word of Order $n$ right after its $w$-th odd step, after $m$ letters, and let
$y = T^m(c)$. Then for every $\lambda$
\[
\rho - \lambda c = \rho_p\Bigl(3^{n-w} + \frac{\lambda}{3^w}\Bigr) + 2^m\Bigl(\sigma - \frac{\lambda\,y}{3^w}\Bigr),
\]
with $\rho_p$ the constant of the start part and $\sigma$ that of the drop part. The two parts are
tied only by $2^m y \equiv \rho_p \pmod{3^w}$, and the start parts are exactly the words of Order
$w$ without their final $0$'s.
\end{mainthm}

\begin{maincomp}[The bests; Section~\ref{sec:search}, Appendix~\ref{app:table}]
The bests of Orders $1$ to $378$ are computed and certified (OEIS A402443\oeisidx{A402443}~\cite{OEIS_A402443}). For $307$ of the Orders $1$ to
$378$ the best is the smallest start of its Order (OEIS A122442\oeisidx{A122442}); at the others the $\theta$ of the smallest start
is more than $1/4$ of the best's (smallest at Order $272$).
\end{maincomp}

\subsection*{The drop zeta function}

\begin{mainthm}[The function; Sections~\ref{sec:zeta} and~\ref{sec:euler}]
$P$ is entire, $P(1) = 1$, and $Z$ is meromorphic on $\C$ without zeros, with a simple pole at
$s = 1$ of residue $1/\mu$, $\mu = \sum_n m_n \ln(2^{p_2(n)}/3^n)$. For $\Rea s > 1$,
$Z(s) = \sum_{k \ge 0} P(s)^k = \prod_\gamma (1 - w_\gamma(s))^{-1}$, the product over the prime
cycles $\gamma$ of the drop map on $\Z_2$.
\end{mainthm}

\begin{mainthm}[The poles; Section~\ref{sec:poles}]
In each band $|\Ima s - 2\pi k/\ln 2| < \pi/\ln 2$, $k \in \Z$, $Z$ has exactly one pole $s_k$, and
it is simple; $s_0 = 1$, and no other pole has $\Rea s \ge 1$. There is one continuous function $z$
on $\mathbb{R}/\Z$ with $s_k = 2\pi i k/\ln 2 + z(k\alpha \bmod 1)$ and
$\Rea z(\beta) = 1 - (2\pi^2 V/\mu)\,\beta^2 + O(\beta^4)$, $V > 0$; hence
$c_1 \|k\alpha\|^2 \le 1 - \Rea s_k \le c_2 \|k\alpha\|^2$ for constants $c_2 \ge c_1 > 0$ and all
$k \ne 0$, and the closure of $\{\Rea s_k : k \ge 1\}$ is $[\min \Rea z, 1]$.
\end{mainthm}

\begin{maincomp}[The fall; Section~\ref{sec:poles}]
$\Rea z$ falls strictly on $[0, 1/2]$ (certified). So $\Rea s_j > \Rea s_k$ exactly when
$\|j\alpha\| < \|k\alpha\|$, the poles nearest to $\Rea s = 1$ come at the convergent denominators
$k = 1, 2, 5, 12, 41, 53, 306, 665, \dots$ of $\log_2 3$, and the real parts of the poles are dense in
$[z(1/2), 1]$, $z(1/2) = [-1; 1, 1, 1, 2, 1, 7, 1, 5, 2, 2, 3, 27, \dots]$.
\end{maincomp}

\begin{mainthm}[The mass per Order; Section~\ref{sec:mass}]
For every $n \ge 0$, with $L = p_2(n)$,
\[
\binom{L}{n} = L\,N(n) + \sum_{m} N(m) \binom{p_2(n-m)}{n-m},
\]
the sum over the $1 \le m < n$ with $p_2(m) + p_2(n-m) = p_2(n)$, that is with
$2^{p_2(m)} 3^n < 2^{p_2(n)} 3^m$. In particular $L\,N(n) \le \binom{L}{n}$, with equality exactly
when $p_2(n) - n\alpha$ is smaller than all $p_2(m) - m\alpha$, $1 \le m < n$: at the upper record
Orders $1, 3, 5, 17, 29, 41, 94, \dots$, where $\theta_n$ is above every earlier floor. The words of
length $L$ with $n$ ones none of whose rotations is a drop word number the sum (OEIS A400061).
Formalized in Lean.
\end{mainthm}
\begin{mainthm}[The jumps; Section~\ref{sec:mass}]
With $p = 1/\alpha$: $\sum_{m \ge 1} N(m)\, p^m (1-p)^{p_2(m) - m} = p$.
\end{mainthm}

\begin{mainthm}[The staircase; Section~\ref{sec:mass}]
With $p = 1/\alpha$ and $G(e) = 1 - \sum_{m \ge 1,\, e_m < e} N(m)\,p^m(1-p)^{p_2(m)-m}$:
$L\,N(n)/\binom{L}{n} - G(e_n) = O(n^{-1/2})$ as $n \to \infty$. So the share of the words with no drop
rotation is, up to $O(n^{-1/2})$, the chance $1 - G(e_n)$ that the first drop of a word with independent
letters, a one with chance $p$, has its floor above $\theta_n$; and the mass is
$m_n = \kappa^n(\alpha/(2(\alpha-1)))^{e_n}\,w(n)$ exactly, $w(n)$ the chance of a first drop at Order $n$.
\end{mainthm}

\begin{mainthm}[The explicit formula; Section~\ref{sec:compare}]
With $U(u)$ the mean number of drops whose slopes multiply to at least $e^{-u}$, $U_1(u) = \int_0^u U$,
\[
U_1(u) = \frac{u^2/2 + c_1 u + c_2}{\mu} + \lim_{K \to \infty}\sum_{0 < |k| \le K}
\frac{e^{(s_k-1)u}}{-P'(s_k)(s_k-1)^2} \qquad (u \ge 0),
\]
with constants $c_1$, $c_2$ from the moments of the $\lambda_n$. $P$ has no zeros with $\Rea s \le 1$.
\end{mainthm}

\subsection*{What was known}
The affine form of $T^L$ on a parity class, the residue classes of the parity words, the density of
the starts that drop and the fact that almost every start drops are due to Terras~\cite{Terras1976}
and Everett~\cite{Everett1977}; the largest $\rho$ of an Order and its unique word are
Terras's~\cite[Thm.~2.6]{Terras1976}.

\emph{The floors.} The three-distance theorem, conjectured by Steinhaus, was proved first by
\'Swierczkowski~\cite{Swierczkowski1959}, then by Erd\H{o}s, Sz\"usz, Haj\'os and S\'os
(Sur\'anyi~\cite[\S1]{Suranyi1958}; S\'os~\cite{Sos1958} gave the order of the points by their adjacent
multiples); Sur\'anyi~\cite{Suranyi1958} gave it by the Farey sequence, and Theorem~A is his, read on
the floors. The record Orders as the semiconvergent denominators of $\alpha$ are in
paper~2~\cite{Paper2}, formalized in Lean. Pythagorean tuning as the three-gap case of the circle of
fifths is classical in music theory: Carey and Clampitt~\cite{CareyClampitt1989} call a scale of $B$
fifths well formed, and show that it is so exactly when $B$ is a convergent or semiconvergent
denominator of $\log_2 3$ (their Characterization Theorem), which is the case of Theorem~A without the
product: $B$ a record Order.

\emph{The best.} The cycle point of a parity word, $\rho/(2^L - 3^n)$, is Böhm and
Sontacchi's~\cite[Prop.~5]{BohmSontacchi1978} and, for every word and over the rationals with odd
denominator, Lagarias's~\cite[Thm.~2.1]{Lagarias1990}. The parametric method for fractional programs
is Dinkelbach's~\cite{Dinkelbach1967}; accelerated versions are in \cite{DadushKNV2021}
and~\cite{ChenZhengXia2025}. The smallest start of each Order is OEIS
A122442\oeisidx{A122442}~\cite{OEIS_A122442}. The admissible words, their tree and the names used here
are those of the series this paper stands beside~\cite{Paper1,Paper2,Paper3}.

\emph{The zeta function.} The bound $L\,N(n) \le \binom{L}{n}$ with its equality case, and a lower
bound by $\binom{L-1}{n}/(L-1)$, are Winkler's~\cite{Winkler2026}, by the cycle lemma; paper~1 of this
series~\cite{Paper1} cites the upper one, and its equality Orders are the upper record Orders of
paper~2~\cite{Paper2}; the rate $O(n^{-1/2})$ in Theorem~K is Winkler's too (Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}).
The prime zeta function and the shape of its zeros, which this paper follows, are in
MathWorld~\cite{MathWorldPZ}; dynamical zeta functions go back to Artin and
Mazur~\cite{ArtinMazur1965}. The cycle lemma and the cycle identity of Section~\ref{sec:mass} are
classical (Feller~\cite{Feller2}).

As far as we know, the following are new: the reading of the three gaps on the floors (the end
ratios as Pythagorean intervals, counted by the last lower and upper record Orders); the question
of the best of the Order, the share and the least point of the cycle in Theorem~B, Theorems~C and~D,
and the table of Computation~E; the function $Z$, Theorems~G, J and~L (the main term of~L is the
renewal theorem~\cite{Feller2}), the identity of Theorem~I with its restricted sum and its reading on
the floors, Theorem~K, and Computation~H.

\clearpage
\tableofcontents

%------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Introduction}
\label{sec:intro}
This paper looks at the drop of a start from three sides. The \emph{floors} (Sections~\ref{sec:picture}
and~\ref{sec:gaps}) bound the drop ratios of each Order from below; the \emph{best of the Order}
(Sections~\ref{sec:wall} to~\ref{sec:observations}) bounds them from above; the octave clock
(Section~\ref{sec:octave}) shows both bounds of every Order on one picture, each Order as a short arc
from its floor to its best; and the drop zeta function (Sections~\ref{sec:zeta} to~\ref{sec:compare})
counts the drops with the drop words as its primes, among which the floors are the powers of
$F = 3/4$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:primes}). Section~\ref{sec:picture} opens in the style of
paper~1~\cite{Paper1}, without proofs.

\medskip
\termhere{drop}Run the map $T(x) = x/2$ for even $x$, $T(x) = (3x+1)/2$ for odd $x$, from a start $x > 1$, and
stop at the \keyterm{drop}{drop}: the first step at which the value falls below $x$. \termhere{admissible}\termhere{order}Even starts drop at once; an odd
start rises first, and its parity \fwdterm{word}{word} up to the drop is an \keyterm{admissible}{admissible} word: $n$ odd steps in
$L = p_2(n)$ steps, \fwdsym{p2}{$p_2(n)$} the bit length of $3^n$, with $3^j > 2^\ell$ after every earlier step
(for every $n$ up to $10\,781\,273$, and for all $n$ if Terras's conjecture holds: Remark~\ref{rem:width}).
This $n$ is the \keyterm{order}{Order} of the drop. \termhere{classid}Each admissible word is the start of one residue class modulo
$2^L$, and its least member above $1$, the \keyterm{classid}{class id} $c$, stands for the whole class
(Section~\ref{sec:words}).

\medskip
\termhere{dropratio}All starts of a class are mapped by one affine map, $2^L T^L(x) = 3^n x + \rho$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:affine}), so the \keyterm{dropratio}{drop ratio}
$T^L(x)/x = 3^n/2^L + \rho/(2^L x)$ is highest at the class id. Every start of Order $n$ falls at least
to $3^n/2^L$ of itself; the \fwdterm{rho}{constant} $\rho$ is what holds it up. \termhere{best}We ask, per Order: which class
id falls the least? We call it the \keyterm{best}{best of the Order}. \termhere{widthidentity}Its ratio is the one closest to $1$, so it
is the hardest case of the statement that every class id drops at its word, Terras's coefficient
stopping time conjecture, in the series called the \keyterm{widthidentity}{width identity}.

\medskip
The answer has a clean form (Theorem~\ref{thm:cycle}): each word has a rational \fwdterm{cyclepoint}{cycle point}
$x^\ast = \rho/(2^L - 3^n)$, the strict minimum of its own rational cycle, and the best is the class id
with the largest \fwdterm{share}{share} $\theta = x^\ast/c$. Since $\rho$ is at most the constant \fwdsym{R}{$R(n)$} of the \fwdterm{wallword}{wall word}
(Section~\ref{sec:wall}), a start can only win inside a \fwdterm{window}{window} (Theorem~\ref{thm:window}); this turns
the question into two exact searches, a \fwdterm{bestfirst}{best-first search} over the word tree and a \fwdterm{scan}{scan} over the starts
in increasing order, both seeded with the \fwdterm{seed}{smallest start} of the Order (OEIS A122442\oeisidx{A122442}).

\medskip
As a fractional program the
question invites \fwdterm{lambdastar}{Dinkelbach's method}; cutting a word after its $w$-th one splits $\rho - \lambda c$ into
a start side and a drop side (the \fwdterm{meetingsplit}{meeting split}), tied only by one congruence modulo $3^w$, and the \fwdterm{startpart}{start sides} are the
words of Order $w$ (Section~\ref{sec:dinkelbach}).

\medskip
Section~\ref{sec:search} gives the searches and their
cost, Section~\ref{sec:observations} what the bests of Orders $1$ to $378$ show, and
Section~\ref{sec:lean} the formalization in Lean. Appendix~\ref{app:failed} lists the ways to guess the
best that did not work, and Appendix~\ref{app:table} the bests.

\medskip
The picture on the title page shows four words of Order $6$ (Remark~\ref{rem:order6}) as turns: the best, the
smallest start, the word with all its ones first, and the \fwdterm{wallword}{wall word}. After $\ell$ letters a word
stands at the distance $T^\ell(c)/c$ from the centre, divided by $2^z$ for the $z$ zeros that end it. So it starts on
the axis at $1/2^z$, and its last $1$ brings it back to the axis at its drop ratio, just inside the circle of
radius~$1$. A $0$ goes straight inward. The $q$-th $1$, at letter $k_q$, turns by its share of the \fwdterm{rho}{constant},
$3^{n-q}\,2^{k_q}/\rho$ of a full turn (Lemma~\ref{lem:affine}), so every word turns once around: a $1$ that follows a $1$ turns
$2/3$ as far as the one before it, and each $0$ between them doubles that. A dot marks the end of each letter, filled after a $1$. The page
\texttt{drop\_turn.html} of the repository draws this picture for the Orders $2$ to $14$, with other scalings.

\medskip
The Lean files, the scripts and the data (the bests with their certificates, the smallest starts) are at
\url{https://github.com/druud/Collatz-but-stop-at-the-drop}, in \texttt{src/lean\_files/}
(\texttt{AcceleratedCollatz/Zeta/} and \texttt{zeta/}).
The script \texttt{paper\_numbers.py} there recomputes the numbers of this paper and compares each
with the text.
The paper, the Lean proofs and the scripts were made with Claude (Anthropic) as a working
partner; Section~\ref{sec:lean} tells how.

\boxhere{admissible}admissible word\index{admissible word@\termlink{admissible}{admissible word}|textbf} & the parity word of a start up to its drop: $n$ odd steps in $L = p_2(n)$ letters, with $3^j > 2^\ell$ after every earlier letter. \\ \hline
\boxhere{order}Order $n$\index{Order@\termlink{order}{Order $n$}|textbf} & the number of odd steps up to the drop. \\ \hline

\boxhere{best}best of the Order\index{best of the Order@\termlink{best}{best of the Order}|textbf} & the class id of Order $n$ with the highest drop ratio: the one that falls the least. \\ \hline
\boxhere{widthidentity}width identity\index{width identity@\termlink{widthidentity}{width identity}|textbf} & every class id $c > 1$ drops at the end of its word (Terras's coefficient stopping time conjecture); it holds for Orders up to $10\,781\,273$~\cite{Paper2}. \\ \hline

\clearpage
\section{The picture}
\label{sec:picture}

% In paper 1's style: what a floor is, read off Figure 1, no proofs.
Pick a whole number and play the game of paper~1~\cite{Paper1}: \emph{even}: halve it;
\emph{odd}: triple it, add one, and halve the result. Stop as soon as you are below the start: the
drop. From $5$: $5 \to 8 \to 4$, one odd step, and $4/5$ of the start is left. Every fourth number
$1, 5, 9, 13, \dots$ plays the same two steps, and $4i + 1$ lands on $3i + 1$. What is left of the start,
\[
\tfrac{1}{1}, \quad \tfrac{4}{5}, \quad \tfrac{7}{9}, \quad \tfrac{10}{13}, \quad \dots,
\quad \tfrac{3i+1}{4i+1}, \quad \dots,
\]
gets smaller and smaller, closer and closer to $3/4$, but never gets there. The family $16i + 3$, with
two odd steps ($3 \to 5 \to 8 \to 4 \to 2$), lands on $9i + 2$: closer and closer to $9/16$. With $n$
odd steps a family keeps at least $3^n/2^L$ of its start, $L$ the number of steps: the \emph{floor} of
the families with $n$ odd steps, their \emph{Order}. The number of steps is fixed by the Order: $2^L$ is
the first power of $2$ above $3^n$. So every floor lies between $1/2$ and $1$: in one octave.

Put one dot per Order on that octave, Order $n$ at height $n$ (Figure~\ref{fig:floordots}). The dots look
scattered, but they lie on lines. Twelve Orders up, a dot moves a little to the right, by the factor
$3^{12}/2^{19}$: the Pythagorean comma of music, as twelve fifths ($3/2$) overshoot seven octaves by it.
Five Orders up, a dot moves to the left, by the factor $2^8/3^5 = 256/243$: the limma, the half tone of
Pythagorean tuning. A red dot is further right than every dot below it: those Orders $1, 3, 5, 17, 29,
\dots$ have the highest floor so far. A blue dot is further left than every dot below it: $1, 2, 7, 12,
\dots$.

Now look at the gaps between neighbouring dots, side by side, of the Orders $0$ to $12$. Measured as
ratios, there are only three sizes: the limma $256/243$, the apotome $3^7/2^{11}$, and the comma, and
the apotome is the limma times the comma. Those are the steps of the Pythagorean scale. For any number
of Orders there are never more than three sizes (Theorem~A), and the red and blue Orders are where a new
size comes in.

The floors also count words. Write a family as its odd and even steps, \texttt{1} and \texttt{0}: the
family $4i + 1$ is \texttt{10}, and $16i + 3$ is \texttt{1100}. For the red Orders, and only for them,
every arrangement of $n$ ones and $L - n$ zeros, read around a circle, can be started at a point where
it reads as a family (Theorem~I).

\caption{The floors $\theta_n = 3^n/2^{p_2(n)}$, Orders $0$ to $40$ (upward), in the octave $[1/2, 1)$.
Filled red: the upper record Orders ($\theta_n$ above every earlier floor); filled blue: the lower
ones ($\theta_n$ below every earlier floor; Order $1$ is both). Dashed: the lines of the comma
($n$, $n + 12$), dotted: of the limma ($n$, $n + 5$).}
\label{fig:floordots}
\end{figure}

%------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Words and Orders}
\label{sec:words}

\begin{definition}[The map, the word, the Order]
$T(x) = x/2$ for even $x$, $T(x) = (3x+1)/2$ for odd $x$. \termhere{word}The \keyterm{word}{word} of $x$ is its parity
sequence $e_0 e_1 e_2 \ldots$, $e_\ell = T^\ell(x) \bmod 2$. \termhere{p2}Let \keysym{p2}{$p_2(n)$} be the bit length of $3^n$,
so $2^{p_2(n)-1} \le 3^n < 2^{p_2(n)}$, with equality only for $n = 0$. A word of length $L$ with $n$ letters $1$ is
\emph{admissible} of \emph{Order} $n$ if
\[
3^{j} > 2^{\ell} \quad\text{for each nonempty proper prefix ($j$ ones among its $\ell$ letters)},
\qquad 3^n < 2^L.
\]
Order $0$ is the word \bw{0} of the even starts, with $L = 1 = p_2(0)$. For $n \ge 1$ again $L = p_2(n)$ and the last letter is $0$: the prefix of
length $L-1$ has $3^{j} > 2^{L-1}$; were the last letter $1$, then $j = n - 1$ and
$3^n = 3 \cdot 3^{n-1} > 3 \cdot 2^{L-1} > 2^L$. So $j = n$ and $2^{L-1} < 3^n < 2^L$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Terras]
\label{lem:affine}
\termhere{rho}Let $e = e_0 e_1 \ldots e_{L-1}$ be a word of length $L$ with $n$ ones, the $q$-th at letter $k_q$ ($0$-based). For every $x$
whose word starts with $e$,
\[
2^L\,T^L(x) = 3^n x + \rho, \qquad \rho = \sum_{q=1}^{n} 3^{\,n-q}\,2^{k_q},
\]
and these $x$ form one residue class modulo $2^L$; \keysym{rho}{$\rho$} is the \emph{constant} of $e$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Induction on $L$; for $L = 0$, $\rho = 0$ and the class is all of $\mathbb{Z}$. Let the statement hold for
$e$ and append a letter $e_L$. If $e_L = 0$, then $T^L(x)$ is even and
$2^{L+1}T^{L+1}(x) = 2^L T^L(x) = 3^n x + \rho$. If $e_L = 1$, then
$2^{L+1}T^{L+1}(x) = 3 \cdot 2^L T^L(x) + 2^L = 3^{n+1}x + 3\rho + 2^L$, and $3\rho + 2^L$ is the constant
with a new one at letter $k_{n+1} = L$. For the classes: writing $x = 2^L i + r$ in the class of $e$,
$T^L(x) = 3^n i + T^L(r)$, whose parity is that of $i + T^L(r)$; so the letter $e_L$ fixes $i \bmod 2$,
and $e$ with $e_L$ appended is one class modulo $2^{L+1}$. \cite[§1]{Terras1976}.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Class id, drop value]
The \emph{class id} of an admissible word $e$ of Order $n$ is the least $c > 1$ whose word starts with $e$
(for the residue $r < 2^L$ of the class: $c = r$ if $r > 1$, else $c = r + 2^L$; Order~$1$ gives $c = 5$).
\termhere{dropvalue}Its \keyterm{dropvalue}{drop value} is $d = T^L(c)$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}
\label{lem:congruence}
$c \equiv -\rho\,3^{-n} = -\sum_{q=1}^n 2^{k_q}\,3^{-q} \pmod{2^L}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
$3^n c + \rho = 2^L T^L(c) \equiv 0 \pmod{2^L}$ by Lemma~\ref{lem:affine}, and $3$ is invertible
modulo $2^L$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
\label{lem:member}
Every $x > 1$ of the class of an admissible word of Order $n \ge 1$, with class id $c$, has $T^L(x)/x \le T^L(c)/c$, with equality only for $x = c$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
By Lemma~\ref{lem:affine},
\[
\frac{T^L(x)}{x} = \frac{3^n}{2^L} + \frac{\rho}{2^L x}
\]
with the same $\rho > 0$ for the whole class, and $x \ge c$ by the choice of $c$.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[Best of the Order]
The \emph{best of Order $n$} is the class id $c$ of Order $n$ with the largest drop ratio
$T^L(c)/c$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:member} it is also the start $x > 1$ of Order $n$ with the largest ratio.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
\label{rem:residue}
The words of all Orders form one binary tree (Figure~\ref{fig:tree}); \cite[Ch.~4]{Paper3}.
For Order $1$ the residue is $1$ and $c = 5$; for $n \ge 2$ the residue of an admissible word is
more than $1$ (the word of $1$ is $\bw{1010}\ldots$, and $\bw{10}$ is not a prefix of a word of Order
$n \ge 2$), so $c = r < 2^L$.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Order $6$]
\label{rem:order6}
Order $6$ has $L = p_2(6) = 10$ and $12$ words (Figure~\ref{fig:tree}). The word \bw{1101110100} has its
ones at the letters $0, 1, 3, 4, 5, 7$, so
\[
\rho = 3^5 + 3^4\cdot 2 + 3^3\cdot 2^3 + 3^2\cdot 2^4 + 3\cdot 2^5 + 2^7 = 989.
\]
Its class id is $c = 347$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:congruence}), and
\[
3^6\cdot 347 + 989 = 253\,952 = 2^{10}\cdot 248,
\]
so $d = 248$ and the drop ratio is $248/347$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:member} the best of the Order is the
word with the largest $\rho/c$: a large constant (ones late) against a small class id. The wall word
\bw{1101101100} (Section~\ref{sec:wall}) has the largest constant, $\rho = 1085$, with $c = 507$; the
word \bw{1111101000} has the smallest class id, $c = 287$ (A122442\oeisidx{A122442}), with $\rho = 697$. Neither wins:
\[
\frac{989}{347} > \frac{697}{287} > \frac{1085}{507},
\]
and these three lead the twelve words of Order $6$; the best is $347$ (\texttt{boo\_count.py}).
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[The number of words]
\label{rem:count}
Counted by ones and letters, the number of words of Order $n$ is
OEIS A100982\oeisidx{A100982}~\cite{OEIS_A100982}:
\[
1,\ 1,\ 2,\ 3,\ 7,\ 12,\ 30,\ 85,\ 173,\ 476 \qquad (n = 1, \ldots, 10).
\]
Order $124$ has a number of words of $53$ digits, Order $378$ one of $168$ digits
(\texttt{boo\_count.py}, exact). So the searches of Section~\ref{sec:search} never list the words of an
Order: they stop at a window on the class ids.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Ties]
\label{rem:ties}
\termhere{tie}Two class ids of one Order can share their drop ratio, a \keyterm{tie}{tie}: the first case is Order $9$, where $21223$ and
$25691$ have the same $\rho/c$; Order $13$ has $16$ class ids with one ratio, and Order $16$ has
$56$ sets of equal ratios. Within an Order the constant fixes the word~\cite[Cor.~2.1a]{Lagarias1990}, so a tie has two different constants. A class id $c$ ties $c^\ast$ only if $\rho c^\ast = \rho^\ast c$, so $c$ is a multiple of
$u = c^\ast/\gcd(c^\ast, \rho^\ast)$; and a tie of the best lies in the window of
Theorem~\ref{thm:window} (with $\le$ for $<$), so $c = ku$ with $k \le W/u$, $W$ the window of $c^\ast$. For Orders $1$ to $378$
that leaves $7582$ candidates (at most $1063$, at Order $262$), and none is a tie
(\texttt{boo\_tie\_mult.py}, exact): the best has no tie for Orders $1$ to $378$. Independently, a
depth-first search of the words with the window of the best finds none for Orders $1$ to $229$
(\texttt{boo\_ties.cpp}). That it never has one is open. Where we say \emph{the} best, a tie would mean the smallest of them.
\end{remark}

\subsection*{Drop words, floors and masses}

The admissible words are the \emph{drop words}: the \emph{level} of a word with $a$ ones among $j$
letters is $3^a/2^j$, and a drop word is a nonempty word whose proper nonempty prefixes all have level
above $1$ and whose level is below $1$. For a word $v$ we write $\lv(v, i)$ for the level of its first
$i$ letters, so $\lv(v, 0) = 1$. Two prefixes of different lengths have different levels, as
$3^a 2^{j'} \ne 3^{a'} 2^{j}$ unless $a = a'$ and $j = j'$. No drop word is a proper prefix of another.
Write $\Drop(n)$ for the drop words of Order $n$ and $N(n) = |\Drop(n)|$ (OEIS
A100982\oeisidx{A100982}~\cite{OEIS_A100982}; $N(0) = 1$, the word $\bw{0}$); the word
$1^n 0^{p_2(n)-n}$ shows $N(n) \ge 1$. With the ones of a drop word $w$ at the letters
$t_1 < \dots < t_n$ counted from $1$ ($t_q = k_q + 1$), its constant is
$\rho_w = \sum_q 3^{n-q} 2^{t_q - 1}$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:affine}), and among the $2$-adic integers its
starts form one class $c + 2^L \Z_2$, $0 \le c < 2^L$. For a drop word, $T^L(x) < x$ for the positive
integers $x$ of the class with $x > \rho_w/(2^L - 3^n)$ (Terras~\cite[Prop.~1.7]{Terras1976}), and
$T^j(x) > x$ for $0 < j < L$ and every positive $x$ of the class.

\begin{definition}
\termhere{floor}The \emph{mass} of Order $n$ is $m_n = N(n)/2^{p_2(n)}$, the $2$-adic measure of the classes of its
drop words; its \keyterm{floor}{floor} is $\theta_n = 3^n/2^{p_2(n)}$, the slope of $T^L$ on them.
\end{definition}

So $\theta_0 = 1/2$ and $1/2 < \theta_n < 1$ for $n \ge 1$; with $e_n = p_2(n) - n\alpha \in (0, 1]$,
$\theta_n = 2^{-e_n}$, and $e_n = 1 - \{n\alpha\}$ for $n \ge 1$. For a start $x$ of a class of Order $n$,
$T^L(x)/x = \theta_n + \rho_w/(2^L x)$ falls strictly towards $\theta_n$ along the class
(Lemma~\ref{lem:member}): the floor is the infimum of the drop ratios of the Order, never attained,
and the drop ratio of the best is their maximum.

\begin{lemma}[Terras]\label{lem:mass}
$\sum_{n \ge 0} m_n = 1$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Read the parities of a $2$-adic integer chosen by the Haar measure: independent fair letters. The
logarithm of the level is a random walk with mean step $\frac12 \log(3/4) < 0$, so it falls below
$0$ almost surely (strong law of large numbers), and the word read up to the first time it does is a
drop word. The events for different drop words are disjoint, of probability $2^{-L}$ each.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}
\termhere{record}An Order $n \ge 1$ is an \keyterm{record}{upper record Order} if $\theta_n > \theta_m$ for all $1 \le m < n$, and a
\emph{lower record Order} if $\theta_n < \theta_m$ for all $1 \le m < n$.
\end{definition}

The upper record Orders are $1, 3, 5, 17, 29, 41, 94, 147, 200, 253, 306, 971, \dots$, the lower ones
$1, 2, 7, 12, 53, 359, 665, \dots$: the denominators of the upper and lower semiconvergents of
$\alpha$ (paper~2~\cite{Paper2}, \lean{upperRecord\_iff\_semiconv}, \lean{lowerRecord\_iff\_semiconv}).

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:p2split}
For $1 \le m < n$: $p_2(m) + p_2(n - m) - p_2(n) \in \{0, 1\}$, and it is $0$ iff $\theta_m > \theta_n$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Put $a = p_2(m)$, $b = p_2(n-m)$. As
$2^{a-1} < 3^m < 2^a$ and $2^{b-1} < 3^{n-m} < 2^b$, $2^{a+b-2} < 3^n < 2^{a+b}$, so $p_2(n)$ is $a + b - 1$
or $a + b$. Next, $\theta_m > \theta_n$ iff $2^{p_2(n)} > 2^a 3^{n-m}$. For $p_2(n) = a + b$ this holds, as
$3^{n-m} < 2^b$; for $p_2(n) = a + b - 1$ it fails, as $3^{n-m} > 2^{b-1}$.
\end{proof}

\nopagebreak

\begin{wordbox}
\boxhere{word}word\index{word@\termlink{word}{word}|textbf} & the parity sequence $e_0 e_1 e_2\ldots$ of a start under $T$; $1$ for an odd step. \\ \hline
\boxhere{p2}$p_2(n)$\index{p2@\termlink{p2}{$p_2(n)$}|textbf} & the bit length of $3^n$: the length $L$ of a word of Order $n$. \\ \hline
\boxhere{rho}constant $\rho$\index{constant rho@\termlink{rho}{constant $\rho$}|textbf} & $\sum_q 3^{n-q}2^{k_q}$ over the ones of a word; $2^L T^L(x) = 3^n x + \rho$ on its class. \\ \hline
\boxhere{dropvalue}drop value $d$\index{drop value@\termlink{dropvalue}{drop value $d$}|textbf} & $T^L(c)$, the value of the class id at its drop. \\ \hline
\boxhere{tie}tie\index{tie@\termlink{tie}{tie}|textbf} & two class ids of one Order with the same drop ratio. \\ \hline
\boxhere{floor}floor $\theta_n$\index{floor@\termlink{floor}{floor $\theta_n$}|textbf} & $3^n/2^{p_2(n)}$: the slope of $T^L$ on the classes of Order $n$, the infimum of their drop ratios. \\ \hline
\boxhere{record}record Order\index{record Order@\termlink{record}{record Order}|textbf} & an Order whose floor is above (upper) or below (lower) all earlier ones. \\ \hline
A100982\oeisidx{A100982} & the number of words of each Order.
\end{wordbox}

\clearpage
\section{The three gaps}
\label{sec:gaps}

Put the octave $[1/2, 1)$ on a circle, $1$ and $1/2$ the same point. For $N \ge 1$, the floors
$\theta_0, \dots, \theta_N$ are distinct and cut the circle into $N + 1$ arcs; the \emph{end ratio} of
an arc from $\theta$ up to the next floor $\theta'$ is $\theta'/\theta$, and of the arc from the
highest floor $\theta$ round to $\theta_0 = 1/2$ it is $1/\theta$. For $N \ge 1$ let $a_N$ and $b_N$ be the
last lower and the last upper record Order up to $N$: $\theta_{a_N}$ is the lowest of
$\theta_1, \dots, \theta_N$, and $\theta_{b_N}$ the highest.

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:gaps}
Let $N \ge 1$, $a = a_N$, $b = b_N$. The end ratios of the floors $\theta_0, \dots, \theta_N$ are
\[
2\theta_a = \frac{3^a}{2^{p_2(a)-1}} \ (N - a + 1 \text{ times}), \qquad
\frac{1}{\theta_b} = \frac{2^{p_2(b)}}{3^b} \ (N - b + 1 \text{ times}), \qquad
\frac{2\theta_a}{\theta_b} \ (a + b - N - 1 \text{ times}).
\]
So there are at most three, the largest the product of the other two, and exactly two iff $N + 1 = a + b$,
iff $N + 1$ is a record Order: $a + b$ is the next record Order after $N$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
On the logarithmic octave, $\theta$ at $\log_2\theta + 1$ of a turn, the floor of Order $n$ sits at
$\{n\alpha\} = \{n\alpha'\}$, $\alpha' = \alpha - 1 \in (0, 1)$, irrational (Order $0$ at $0$), and the end ratio
of an arc is $2$ to the power of its length. Let $l/k < \alpha' < l'/k'$ be the neighbours of $\alpha'$ in the
Farey sequence of order $N$. By Sur\'anyi~\cite[Satz~I]{Suranyi1958}, $\{k\alpha'\}$ is the least and
$\{k'\alpha'\}$ the largest of $\{\alpha'\}, \dots, \{N\alpha'\}$: $k = a$ and $k' = b$. By his Satz~II, the point
after $P_m = \{m\alpha'\}$ ($0 \le m \le N$) on the circle is $P_{m+a}$ for $m \le N - a$, $P_{m-b}$ for $m \ge b$,
and $P_{m+a-b}$ for $N - a < m < b$; the arcs have the lengths of the arcs from $P_0$ to $P_a$, from $P_b$
to $P_0$, and from $P_b$ through $P_0$ to $P_a$~\cite[\S7]{Suranyi1958}, with the end ratios
$\theta_a/\theta_0 = 2\theta_a$, $1/\theta_b$, and their product. The three ranges of $m$ have
$N - a + 1$, $N - b + 1$ and $a + b - N - 1 \ge 0$ members. The Farey neighbours of $\alpha'$ stay the same
until the order reaches $a + b$, where the mediant $(l + l')/(a + b)$ comes in between them; so the
least or the largest of the $\{n\alpha'\}$ changes first at $n = a + b$: the next record Order after $N$ is
$a + b$.
\end{proof}

So the gaps change only at the record Orders: at a lower record Order $N$ the ratio $2\theta_N$ comes
in, at an upper one $1/\theta_N$, and the product of the two current ones disappears just before the next
record Order. \'Swierczkowski's Corollary~1~\cite{Swierczkowski1959} has the same three lengths, the third
only for $N < a + b - 1$ (his $a_r$ and $a_k$ are our $a$ and $b$), and S\'os~\cite[\S4]{Sos1958} the same counts in terms of the adjacent multiples of
$\alpha'$, the convergent and semiconvergent denominators~\cite[\S5]{Sos1958}: the record Orders of
paper~2~\cite{Paper2}. Table~\ref{tab:gaps} shows the first changes.

\begin{remark}\label{rem:unimod}
The last records $a = a_N$, $b = b_N$ satisfy $a\,p_2(b) - b\,(p_2(a) - 1) = 1$. For $N = 1$, $a = b = 1$.
At the next record Order $c = a + b$: if it is a lower one, $\theta_c = \theta_a\theta_b$,
$p_2(c) = p_2(a) + p_2(b)$, and the new pair $(c, b)$ gives $c\,p_2(b) - b\,(p_2(c) - 1) = a\,p_2(b) -
b\,(p_2(a) - 1)$; if an upper one, $\theta_c = 2\theta_a\theta_b$, $p_2(c) = p_2(a) + p_2(b) - 1$, and
$(a, c)$ gives $a\,p_2(c) - c\,(p_2(a) - 1) = a\,p_2(b) - b\,(p_2(a) - 1)$ again. So successive upper
records $u < v$ have $v\,p_2(u) - u\,p_2(v) = 1$: the vectors $(u, p_2(u))$ and $(v, p_2(v))$ span the
integer lattice.
\end{remark}

\section{The wall}
\label{sec:wall}

\begin{definition}[The wall word]
\termhere{wallword}The \keyterm{wallword}{wall word} of Order $n$ puts its $q$-th one at letter $p_2(q-1) - 1$, the last place
that keeps the prefix before it admissible.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Terras]
\label{thm:wall}
\termhere{R}For every admissible word of Order $n$, $k_q \le p_2(q-1) - 1$ for each $q$, hence
\[
\rho \le \keysym{R}{R(n)} = \sum_{q=1}^{n} 3^{\,n-q}\,2^{p_2(q-1)-1},
\]
with equality only for the wall word.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
$k_1 = 0 = p_2(0) - 1$. For $q \ge 2$ the prefix before the $q$-th one is nonempty and proper, with
$q-1$ ones in $k_q$ letters, so $2^{k_q} < 3^{q-1} < 2^{p_2(q-1)}$ and $k_q \le p_2(q-1) - 1$. Hence
$R(n) - \rho = \sum_q 3^{n-q}\bigl(2^{p_2(q-1)-1} - 2^{k_q}\bigr) \ge 0$, with equality only if every
$k_q$ is on the wall. The wall word is admissible: a nonempty proper prefix of length $\ell$ with $j$
ones has $\ell \le p_2(j) - 1$ (the next one, at $p_2(j)-1$, is not in it; for $j = n$, $\ell < p_2(n)$),
so $2^\ell \le 2^{p_2(j)-1} < 3^j$. \cite[Thm.~2.6]{Terras1976}; \cite[Thm.~A]{Paper3}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[The tail bound]
\label{cor:tail}
\termhere{tailbound}If a word of Order $n$ starts with a prefix that has $j$ ones and constant $\rho_j$, then
$\rho \le 3^{n-j}\rho_j + \sum_{q>j} 3^{\,n-q}\,2^{p_2(q-1)-1} = 3^{n-j}\rho_j + R(n) - 3^{n-j}R(j)$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
$\rho = 3^{n-j}\rho_j + \sum_{q>j} 3^{n-q}2^{k_q}$, and $k_q \le p_2(q-1)-1$ by Theorem~\ref{thm:wall}.
The terms $q \le j$ of $R(n)$ sum to $3^{n-j}R(j)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[The lower wall]
\label{prop:lowerwall}
For every word with $n$ ones, $\rho \ge 3^n - 2^n$, with equality only for the words that have all
their ones first; of Order $n$ that is the one word of $n$ ones and then $L - n$ zeros. For every
Order $n \ge 1$, $R(n) \le n\,3^{n-1}$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The $q$-th one has $q - 1$ ones before it, so $k_q \ge q - 1$ and
$\rho \ge \sum_{q=1}^{n} 3^{n-q}\,2^{q-1} = 3^n - 2^n$, with equality only if every $k_q = q - 1$. That
word is admissible: $3^j > 2^j$ on the ones, and $3^n > 2^\ell$ for $\ell < L$. On the other side
$2^{p_2(q-1)-1} \le 3^{q-1}$, so each term of $R(n)$ is at most $3^{n-1}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[The start parts]
\label{lem:startparts}
\termhere{startpart}Let $1 \le w < n$. The prefixes of the words of Order $n$ that end in their $w$-th one are exactly the
words of Order $w$ without their final $0$'s.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Let $p$ be such a prefix, of length $m$. As $w < n$ it is a nonempty proper prefix, so $3^w > 2^m$,
and so are its own nonempty prefixes. Append $0$'s up to the least length $m + k$ with
$3^w < 2^{m+k}$: every prefix on the way keeps $3^w > 2^{m+i}$, so this is a word of Order $w$, and $p$
is that word without its final $0$'s. Conversely let $u$ be a word of Order $w$ and $p$ the word $u$
without its final $0$'s, of length $m$, ending in the $w$-th one. Then $p$ is a proper prefix of $u$,
so $3^w > 2^m$. Append $n - w$ ones: each new prefix has $3^{w+i} > 2^{m+i}$. Then append $0$'s up to
the least length with $3^n < 2^\ell$. Every nonempty proper prefix of the result is admissible, so it
is a word of Order $n$, and $p$ is its prefix ending in its $w$-th one. Cf.~\cite[Ch.~4]{Paper3}.
\end{proof}

\nopagebreak

\begin{wordbox}
\boxhere{wallword}wall word\index{wall word@\termlink{wallword}{wall word}|textbf} & the word of Order $n$ with each one as late as admissibility allows: its $q$-th one at letter $p_2(q-1)-1$. \\ \hline
\boxhere{R}$R(n)$\index{R@\termlink{R}{$R(n)$}|textbf} & the constant of the wall word, the largest $\rho$ of Order $n$. \\ \hline
\boxhere{tailbound}tail bound\index{tail bound@\termlink{tailbound}{tail bound}|textbf} & the largest $\rho$ below a prefix: the prefix followed by the rest of the wall word (Corollary~\ref{cor:tail}). \\ \hline
\boxhere{startpart}start part\index{start part@\termlink{startpart}{start part}|textbf} & the prefix of a word up to its $w$-th one: a word of Order $w$ without its final $0$'s.
\end{wordbox}

%------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{The cycle point}
\label{sec:cycle}

\begin{definition}[Cycle point, share]
\label{def:cycle}
\termhere{cyclepoint}\termhere{share}For a word of Order $n$ let \keysym{cyclepoint}{$x^\ast$} $= \rho/(2^L - 3^n)$, the rational fixed point of
$x \mapsto (3^n x + \rho)/2^L$~\cite[Prop.~5]{BohmSontacchi1978}, \cite[Thm.~2.1]{Lagarias1990}, above which a start of the word drops at its end~\cite[Prop.~1.7]{Terras1976}, and for its class id $c$ let \keysym{share}{$\theta$} $= x^\ast/c$, its \emph{share}.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}
\label{thm:cycle}
$1 - T^L(c)/c = (1 - 3^n/2^L)(1 - \theta)$. Hence the best of the Order is the class id with
the largest $\theta$, and $T^L(c) < c$ exactly when $c > x^\ast$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By Lemma~\ref{lem:affine}, $1 - T^L(c)/c = 1 - 3^n/2^L - \rho/(2^L c)$, and
$(1 - 3^n/2^L)\,\theta = \frac{2^L - 3^n}{2^L}\cdot\frac{\rho}{(2^L - 3^n)\,c} = \rho/(2^L c)$. As
$1 - 3^n/2^L > 0$, the ratio $T^L(c)/c$ grows with $\theta$, and $T^L(c) < c$ exactly when $\theta < 1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
\label{rem:width}
That every class id $c > 1$ of every Order satisfies $c > x^\ast$ is the width identity of the series
(Terras's coefficient stopping time conjecture~\cite[Conj.~2.9]{Terras1976}):
open in general, and the best of the Order is its hardest case. Terras proved it for the words of
length up to $2592$~\cite[p.~252]{Terras1976}, which covers the Orders of this paper
($p_2(378) = 600$); with a range check it holds for every Order up to
$10\,781\,273$~\cite{Paper2}. Before letter $L$ every start of the word
rises: $T^\ell(x) = (3^{w_\ell}x + \rho_\ell)/2^\ell > x$ for $0 < \ell < L$. So each start $x > 1$ of an
Order $n \le 10\,781\,273$ first falls below itself exactly at step $L = p_2(n)$.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[The cycle]
\label{prop:cyclemin}
Let $x^\ast_\ell = (3^{w_\ell} x^\ast + \rho_\ell)/2^\ell$ be the points of the rational cycle of the
word, $w_\ell$ and $\rho_\ell$ the ones and the constant of its first $\ell$ letters. Then
$x^\ast_\ell > x^\ast$ for $0 < \ell < L$: the cycle point is the strict minimum of its cycle.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
$x^\ast_L = x^\ast$ by the choice of $x^\ast$, and $x^\ast_{\ell+1}$ is $x^\ast_\ell/2$ or
$(3x^\ast_\ell + 1)/2$ by the letter $e_\ell$ (as in Lemma~\ref{lem:affine}): the points form a cycle of
the two affine maps. For $0 < \ell < L$,
$x^\ast_\ell - x^\ast = \bigl((3^{w_\ell} - 2^\ell)x^\ast + \rho_\ell\bigr)/2^\ell > 0$, since
$3^{w_\ell} > 2^\ell$ for the nonempty proper prefixes, $x^\ast > 0$ and $\rho_\ell > 0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[The split of the trajectory]
\label{lem:trajsplit}
For $0 \le \ell \le L$: $T^\ell(c) = x^\ast_\ell + (c - x^\ast)\,3^{w_\ell}/2^\ell$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
$T^\ell(c) = (3^{w_\ell}c + \rho_\ell)/2^\ell$ by Lemma~\ref{lem:affine}. Both sides are affine in $c$
with slope $3^{w_\ell}/2^\ell$ and take the value $x^\ast_\ell$ at $c = x^\ast$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Prefix cycle points]
\label{prop:prefixneg}
The cycle point of the prefix of length $\ell$ as a word of its own,
$\rho_\ell/(2^\ell - 3^{w_\ell})$, is negative for every nonempty proper prefix.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
$\rho_\ell > 0$ and $2^\ell < 3^{w_\ell}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[A ceiling]
\label{prop:ceiling}
\termhere{ceiling}Let \keysym{ceiling}{$t_n$} be the least integer $x \ge 1$ with $(3x+1)^n < 2^L x^n$: the constant start of a word whose
odd steps all multiply by $3 + 1/x$. Every word of Order $n \ge 1$ has $2^L \le (3 + 1/x^\ast)^n$, so
$x^\ast < t_n$; in particular $x^\ast(\text{best}) \le x^\ast(\text{wall}) = R(n)/(2^L - 3^n) < t_n$. $t_n$ depends on $L/n$ only.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
An even letter halves $x^\ast_\ell$; an odd one takes it to $(3x^\ast_\ell + 1)/2 = x^\ast_\ell\,(3 + 1/x^\ast_\ell)/2$,
at most $x^\ast_\ell\,(3 + 1/x^\ast)/2$ as $x^\ast_\ell \ge x^\ast > 0$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:cyclemin}). Around
the cycle, $x^\ast = x^\ast_L \le x^\ast (3 + 1/x^\ast)^n/2^L$. As $(3 + 1/t_n)^n < 2^L$, $3 + 1/t_n < 3 + 1/x^\ast$.
\end{proof}

\nopagebreak

\begin{wordbox}
\boxhere{cyclepoint}cycle point $x^\ast$\index{cycle point@\termlink{cyclepoint}{cycle point $x^\ast$}|textbf} & $\rho/(2^L - 3^n)$, the rational fixed point of the affine map of a word; the least point of its rational cycle. \\ \hline
\boxhere{share}share $\theta$\index{share theta@\termlink{share}{share $\theta$}|textbf} & $x^\ast/c$; the best of the Order is the class id with the largest share. \\ \hline
\boxhere{ceiling}ceiling $t_n$\index{ceiling@\termlink{ceiling}{ceiling $t_n$}|textbf} & the least $x \ge 1$ with $(3x+1)^n < 2^L x^n$; above the cycle point of every word of Order $n$.
\end{wordbox}

%------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Dinkelbach and the meeting split}
\label{sec:dinkelbach}

By Theorem~\ref{thm:cycle} the best maximizes $\rho/c$ over the words of Order $n$: a fractional
program over a finite set.

\begin{lemma}[Dinkelbach~\cite{Dinkelbach1967}]
\label{lem:dinkelbach}
\termhere{lambdastar}Let \keysym{lambdastar}{$\lambda^\ast$} be the largest $\rho/c$ over the words of Order $n$, and
\[
F(\lambda) = \max\,(\rho - \lambda c),
\]
the maximum over the same words. Then $F$ is strictly decreasing, $F(\lambda^\ast) = 0$, and from any class id the step
$\lambda \mapsto \rho/c$ of an argmax of $\rho - \lambda c$ reaches $\lambda^\ast$ in finitely many steps.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
$F$ is a maximum of finitely many strictly decreasing functions of $\lambda$ ($c > 0$), so it is strictly
decreasing; $\rho - \lambda^\ast c \le 0$ for every word, with equality at the best, so $F(\lambda^\ast) = 0$.
Let $\lambda = \rho_0/c_0 \le \lambda^\ast$ be the ratio of some class id. Then $F(\lambda) \ge 0$. If
$F(\lambda) = 0$, then $\lambda = \lambda^\ast$. Otherwise an argmax has $\rho - \lambda c > 0$, so its
$\rho/c > \lambda$: the steps strictly increase within a finite set, and stop at $\lambda^\ast$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[The steps]
\label{rem:steps}
In Order $6$ (Remark~\ref{rem:order6}) one step suffices from either end. From the smallest start,
$\lambda = 697/287$ gives
\[
F(697/287) = \frac{1024}{7} > 0,
\]
with the argmax $347$, and $F(989/347) = 0$; from the wall word, $\lambda = 1085/507$ gives the same
argmax. Order $13$ ($8045$ words) takes four steps from its wall word,
\[
941\,051 \to 40\,699 \to 6\,427 \to 319 \to 159,
\]
and none from its smallest start $159$, which is the best. For Orders $2$ to $16$ the smallest start
needs at most one step (Orders $6$, $8$, $14$), the wall word at most four (Orders $13$, $16$)
(\texttt{boo\_count.py}, all words, an argmax tie taken at the smaller class id). This is the reason to
seed with $s_0$ (Remark~\ref{rem:seeds}).
\end{remark}

\begin{lemma}[The walk]
\label{lem:walk}
\termhere{walk}Let $n \ge 2$. With $c = \sum_\ell t_\ell 2^\ell$ ($t_\ell$ the bits of $c < 2^L$) and $e_\ell$ the letters,
\[
\rho - \lambda c = \sum_{\ell=0}^{L-1} 2^\ell \delta_\ell,\qquad \delta_\ell = e_\ell\,3^{\,n-q_\ell} - \lambda\,t_\ell,
\]
$q_\ell$ the ones among the letters $0..\ell$ (so $q_\ell = w_{\ell+1}$).
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A one at letter $\ell = k_q$ has $q_\ell = q$, so $\rho = \sum_\ell e_\ell\,3^{n-q_\ell}2^\ell$ by
Lemma~\ref{lem:affine}; $c < 2^L$ by Remark~\ref{rem:residue}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The meeting split]
\label{thm:split}
\termhere{meetingsplit}Cut a word of Order $n$ after its $w$-th one, after $m$ letters; let $\rho_p$ be the constant of the start
part, $\sigma$ that of the drop part (its own letters counted from $0$) and $y = T^m(c)$. Then
\[
\rho = 3^{n-w}\rho_p + 2^m\sigma, \qquad c = \frac{2^m y - \rho_p}{3^w},
\]
and
\[
\rho - \lambda c = \rho_p\Bigl(3^{n-w} + \frac{\lambda}{3^w}\Bigr) + 2^m\Bigl(\sigma - \frac{\lambda\,y}{3^w}\Bigr).
\]
Conversely, let $p$ be a start part (a word of Order $w$ without its final $0$'s,
Lemma~\ref{lem:startparts}) and $v$ a drop part with $n - w$ ones. Then $pv$ is a word of Order $n$
exactly when each nonempty proper prefix of $v$, with $j$ ones in $\ell$ letters, has
\[
3^{w+j} > 2^{m+\ell}, \qquad\text{and}\qquad 3^n < 2^{m+|v|}.
\]
Its members $x > 0$ are then exactly the numbers
$(2^m y - \rho_p)/3^w$ with $y > \rho_p/2^m$ in the class of $v$ and $2^m y \equiv \rho_p \pmod{3^w}$;
the class id is the least of them above $1$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The ones of $v$ sit at $k = m + k'$ and are the $(w+q')$-th of $pv$, so Lemma~\ref{lem:affine} gives
\[
\rho = 3^{n-w}\rho_p + \sum_{q'} 3^{(n-w)-q'}2^{m+k'} = 3^{n-w}\rho_p + 2^m\sigma,
\]
and for the start
part $2^m y = 3^w c + \rho_p$. Substituting $c$ gives the identity. Admissibility of $pv$: the nonempty
prefixes of $pv$ inside $p$ are admissible as $p$ is a proper prefix of a word of Order $w$ with
$3^w > 2^m$, and the longer ones are the condition on $v$.

For the members: if $x$ has the word $pv$,
then $y = T^m(x)$ has the word $v$ and $3^w x = 2^m y - \rho_p$. Conversely, for such a $y$ the number
\[
x = \frac{2^m y - \rho_p}{3^w}
\]
is a positive integer with $3^w x \equiv -\rho_p \pmod{2^m}$, so $x$ is in the
class of $p$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:congruence}, whose proof holds for any word), and
$T^m(x) = (3^w x + \rho_p)/2^m = y$ has the word $v$. The congruence is the only tie between the two
parts: $p$ alone fixes $\rho_p$, $m$, $w$; $v$ alone fixes $\sigma$ and the class of $y$.
Checked exactly by \texttt{mitm.py}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Order $6$, cut at $w = 3$]
\label{rem:splitex}
The best of Order $6$, $c = 347$ with the word \bw{1101110100} (Remark~\ref{rem:order6}), cut after its
third one: the start part is $p = \bw{1101}$, $m = 4$, and the drop part $v = \bw{110100}$. Both have
their ones at the letters $0, 1, 3$, so $\rho_p = \sigma = 3^2 + 3\cdot 2 + 2^3 = 23$, and
\[
\rho = 3^3\cdot 23 + 2^4\cdot 23 = 989, \qquad y = T^4(347) = 587, \qquad c = \frac{2^4\cdot 587 - 23}{3^3} = 347.
\]
The start part is the word \bw{11010} of Order $3$ (class id $11$, and $347 \equiv 11 \pmod{2^4}$)
without its final $0$; the drop part alone asks $y \equiv 11 \pmod{2^6}$, and the two meet in
$2^4 \cdot 587 = 9392 \equiv 23 \pmod{3^3}$. What the split is for: the start sides at a cut are the
words of one lower Order, the same for every $n > w$, and each side can be bounded without the other;
the searches of Section~\ref{sec:search} do not use it yet.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Both sides in turns]
By Lemma~\ref{lem:startparts} the start side at cut $w$ is the set of words of Order $w$, the same
for every $n > w$. The drop side grows backward from the last letter on residues $y \bmod 2^k$
($y' = 2y$ or $(2y-1)/3$). A word can be the best only if each part plus the other side's maximum
is $\ge 0$; with the true maxima, $1$ to $3$ words pass both tests at each cut for Orders $4$ to $13$
(\texttt{mitm4.py}), and $1$ or $2$ for Orders $14$ to $26$ (all words, \texttt{both\_sides.cpp}).
\end{remark}

\nopagebreak

\begin{wordbox}
\boxhere{lambdastar}$\lambda^\ast$, $F(\lambda)$\index{lambda star@\termlink{lambdastar}{$\lambda^\ast$, $F(\lambda)$}|textbf} & the largest $\rho/c$ of Order $n$, and $F(\lambda) = \max(\rho - \lambda c)$, zero exactly at $\lambda^\ast$ (Dinkelbach). \\ \hline
\boxhere{walk}walk $\delta_\ell$\index{walk@\termlink{walk}{walk $\delta_\ell$}|textbf} & $\rho - \lambda c$ letter by letter: $\delta_\ell = e_\ell 3^{n-q_\ell} - \lambda t_\ell$, with $q_\ell$ the ones among the letters $0..\ell$ and $t_\ell$ the bits of $c$. \\ \hline
\boxhere{meetingsplit}meeting split\index{meeting split@\termlink{meetingsplit}{meeting split}|textbf} & a word cut after its $w$-th one into a start part and a drop part (constant $\sigma$), tied only by a congruence modulo $3^w$.
\end{wordbox}

%------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{The search}
\label{sec:search}

\begin{theorem}[The window]
\label{thm:window}
\termhere{window}Let $g/h > 3^n/2^L$ be the drop ratio of some class id of Order $n \ge 1$. A class id $c$ of Order $n$ with
$T^L(c)/c > g/h$ satisfies $c\,(2^L g - 3^n h) < R(n)\,h$, that is, $c < W = \lceil R(n)\,h/(2^L g - 3^n h) \rceil$, the window of that class id. For a prefix with $j$ ones, constant $\rho_j$ and
residue $r$, every class id below it with a higher ratio satisfies
$r\,(2^L g - 3^n h) < \bigl(3^{n-j}\rho_j + \sum_{q>j} 3^{\,n-q} 2^{p_2(q-1)-1}\bigr)\,h$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
$T^L(c)/c > g/h$ means $h\,2^L T^L(c) > g\,2^L c$, that is $h(3^n c + \rho) > 2^L g\,c$, that is
$c\,(2^L g - 3^n h) < \rho\,h$. Now $\rho \le R(n)$ by Theorem~\ref{thm:wall}; below a prefix,
$\rho$ is at most the bound of Corollary~\ref{cor:tail}, and $c \ge r$ as $c \equiv r \pmod{2^\ell}$,
$c > 0$, $0 < r < 2^\ell$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Where the best lies]
\label{cor:bestrange}
Let $s_0$ be the smallest class id above $1$ of Order $n$, with constant $\rho_0$, and $c^\ast$ the best. Then
\[
s_0 \;\le\; c^\ast \;\le\; \frac{R(n)}{\rho_0}\,s_0 \;\le\; \frac{R(n)}{3^n - 2^n}\,s_0 ,
\qquad\text{and}\qquad c^\ast < \frac{n}{2}\,s_0 \quad (n \ge 3).
\]
For $n \ge 3$ also $\theta(s_0) \ge 2\,\theta(c^\ast)/n$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
If $c^\ast \ne s_0$, its ratio is above that of $s_0$ (a tie would make $s_0$ the best), and
Theorem~\ref{thm:window} with $g/h$ the ratio of $s_0$ has $2^L g - 3^n h = \rho_0$, so
$c^\ast \rho_0 < R(n)\,s_0$. Then $\rho_0 \ge 3^n - 2^n$ and $R(n) \le n\,3^{n-1}$ by
Proposition~\ref{prop:lowerwall}, and $2\cdot 3^{n-1} \le 3^n - 2^n$ for $n \ge 3$. The shares:
$\theta(s_0)/\theta(c^\ast) = \rho_0\,c^\ast/(\rho^\ast s_0) \ge \rho_0/R(n) \ge 2/n$ by the same two bounds and $c^\ast \ge s_0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[The size of that range]
\label{rem:bestrange}
The factor $R(n)/(3^n - 2^n)$ is below $31$ at Order $124$ and below $92$ at Order $378$. The bests
found stay far inside: for Orders $1$ to $378$ the largest $c^\ast/s_0$ is $1\,097\,165/347\,477$, between
$3$ and $16/5$, at Order $107$ (\texttt{paper\_numbers.py}).
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[Two exact searches]
\begin{enumerate}
\item \termhere{bestfirst}\keyterm{bestfirst}{Best-first.} Pop the prefixes by residue, prune by Theorem~\ref{thm:window}, stop at the first
residue beyond the window of the best found (\texttt{boo.py}).
\item \termhere{scan}\keyterm{scan}{The scan.} Run over the starts $x$ in increasing order, keep per Order the highest
$T^L(x)/x$; Order $n$ is final once every $x$ below the window of its best is scanned
(\texttt{boo\_scan.cpp}).
\end{enumerate}
Both are exact for any seed; the seed only sets the first window.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Best-first: a prefix of residue $r$ has only descendants of residue at least $r$, and its class ids are
at least $r$, so a popped residue beyond the window of the best found bounds every node still in the
heap; a pruned node holds no class id with a higher ratio by Theorem~\ref{thm:window}; the window only
shrinks as the best improves. The scan computes the first step at which $x$ falls below itself, and
the ones before it; by the remark after Theorem~\ref{thm:cycle} that is the Order of $x$'s word for
all Orders here. (\texttt{boo\_scan.cpp} now also counts the prefixes with $3^j < 2^\ell$ at which $x$ has
not fallen below itself; a run with a count of $0$ does not rest on that remark. The run to Order $378$
was repeated with that count, 46~hours on 6~threads: the count is $0$, with the same best, drop value and
window for every Order; its output is \texttt{data/boo\_scan\_378.out}. So the bests of this paper do not
rest on the remark.) So every class id $c$ of Order $n$ is met as a start of Order $n$, and every other start of its class is larger with a lower ratio (Lemma~\ref{lem:member}); so once
all $x$ below the window of the best found are scanned, Theorem~\ref{thm:window} leaves no class id that
could beat it.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Seeds]
\label{rem:seeds}
\termhere{seed}The smallest start of Order $n$, \keysym{seed}{$s_0$} $= a(n+1)$ of A122442\oeisidx{A122442}, is the best itself for $98$ of the Orders $1$
to $124$ (and for $307$ of the Orders $1$ to $378$). A guess from the previous Order (its $\theta$, halved until a word beats it) is exact as
well but costs about twice the nodes, and certified candidates built from the bests of earlier Orders
beat $s_0$ only at Orders $6$, $8$ and $107$ (Appendix~\ref{app:failed}).
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Cost]
For Orders $1$ to $124$ the best-first search seeded with $s_0$ visits $164\,801\,730$ nodes in all
(Order $124$: $15\,147\,961$); the scan finishes Orders $1$ to $124$ at $x < 2^{26}$. The scan for
Orders $1$ to $378$ covers every start below $886\,522\,563\,681\,567$, the window of Order $378$ and the
largest of these Orders (Theorem~\ref{thm:window}), on a wheel modulo $2^{28}$ that leaves $3\,524\,586$ residues; Orders
$379$ to $399$ would need it up to $17\,216\,187\,145\,311\,549$ (the windows of their seeds; the scan was stopped at Order $378$). Without a seed, the scan finds the smallest start of each
Order on the way, and its first start of each Order is checked against A122442.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[The certificates]
\label{rem:cert}
The certificate of an Order is the line $n$, $c$, $T^L(c)$, $W$. Checking it shows that $c$ is a
class id of Order $n$ with that drop value, and that every class id with a higher ratio is below
$W$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:window}); that there is none is the search itself. This check is done in Lean
for all $378$ lines, by the kernel (\lean{cert\_rows}, Section~\ref{sec:lean}). Orders $1$ to $124$ were
searched twice, by the best-first search and by the scan; Orders $125$ to $229$ by the scan and by a
depth-first search of the words inside $W$ (\texttt{boo\_ties.cpp}: none better, none equal); Orders
$230$ to $378$ by the scan alone.
\end{remark}

\nopagebreak

\begin{wordbox}
\boxhere{window}window $W$\index{window@\termlink{window}{window $W$}|textbf} & the bound $c\,(2^L g - 3^n h) < R(n)\,h$, or $c < W$, that a class id must meet to beat the drop ratio $g/h$ of another (Theorem~\ref{thm:window}). \\ \hline
\boxhere{bestfirst}best-first search\index{best-first search@\termlink{bestfirst}{best-first search}|textbf} & the prefixes popped by residue and pruned by the window (\texttt{boo.py}). \\ \hline
\boxhere{scan}scan\index{scan@\termlink{scan}{scan}|textbf} & all starts in increasing order, up to the window of the best found (\texttt{boo\_scan.cpp}). \\ \hline
\boxhere{seed}seed $s_0$\index{seed@\termlink{seed}{seed $s_0$}|textbf} & the smallest start of the Order (A122442), the first candidate of both searches.
\end{wordbox}

\section{Observations}
\label{sec:observations}
% Data only, measured; each with its script.

\begin{observation}[The smallest start]
$\theta(s_0)/\theta(\text{best}) > 1/4$ for all Orders $1$ to $378$, smallest at Order $272$ (between
$25/100$ and $26/100$), then Orders $361$, $363$, $256$ and $91$; equality of $s_0$ and the best at $307$ of them
(\texttt{boo\_obs.py}).
\end{observation}

\begin{observation}[Backward ranking]
At $\lambda = \lambda^\ast$ let $S_k = \sum_{\ell=L-k}^{L-1} 2^\ell \delta_\ell$ be the sum of the walk over the
last $k$ letters. \termhere{backward}The best is the strict maximum of $S_k$ over all words of its Order for every
$k \ge$ \keysym{backward}{$k_0$}, with $k_0$ in Table~\ref{tab:k0} (Orders $2$ to $21$, all words, \texttt{dinkel\_suffix.py}; $22$ to $28$, \texttt{paper\_numbers\_slow --k0=28}).
For Orders $15$ to $28$, $L/2 < k_0 < 4L/5$; Order $14$ needs $19$ of its $23$ letters.
\end{observation}

\begin{herefloat}{table}
\[

\hline

\hline

k_0 & 1&1&3&2&6&4&8&7&9\\
\hline
\hline

\hline

\caption{Backward ranking: the least $k_0$ from which the best leads the last $k$ letters of the walk.}

\begin{observation}[Merging trajectories]
The drop values $d = T^L(\text{best})$ of Orders $1$ to $378$ have $240$ odd parts: $61$ is the odd
part for $10$ Orders, $12\,729\,682\,859$ for $9$, $619\,739$, $92\,485\,801$, $1\,064\,232\,949$ and
$30\,643\,528\,277$ for $5$ each, $8$ more for $4$ each, $18$ for $3$ each, $45$ for $2$ each, and $163$
for one Order each. Seven of the odd parts
($31$, $167$, $325$, $61$, $23$, $5$, $1$) lie on the trajectory of $27$, for $21$ Orders (\texttt{boo\_hubs.py}). The inverse trees of such hubs do not hold the bests
more often than other starts (Appendix~\ref{app:failed}).
\end{observation}

\subsection*{Open questions}
\begin{enumerate}\setlength{\itemsep}{0.2ex}
\item Does the best of an Order ever have a tie (Remark~\ref{rem:ties}; none for Orders $1$ to $378$)?
\item Is $\theta(s_0)/\theta(\text{best}) > 1/4$ for every Order, or bounded below by a constant at all (it is at least $2/n$, Corollary~\ref{cor:bestrange})?
\item Is the smallest start the best for infinitely many Orders ($307$ of the first $378$)?
\item Does $L/2 < k_0 < 4L/5$ hold beyond Order $28$ (Table~\ref{tab:k0})?
\item At the true maxima, does the drop side of the meeting split keep a bounded number of words
  ($1$ to $3$ measured, Orders $4$ to $26$)? A search on the split would then be a third, independent one.
\item The width identity itself (Remark~\ref{rem:width}).
\end{enumerate}

\begin{wordbox}
\boxhere{backward}backward ranking $k_0$\index{backward ranking@\termlink{backward}{backward ranking $k_0$}|textbf} & the least $k_0$ such that the best leads the walk's sum over the last $k$ letters for every $k \ge k_0$.
\end{wordbox}

\clearpage
\section{The octave clock}
\label{sec:octave}

The ratios $T^L(x)/x$ of the starts $x$ of Order $n \ge 1$ lie in $(\theta_n, 1)$, inside one octave
$[1/2, 1)$. Bend the octave into a circle, $1$ and $1/2$ the same point; Order $n$ on its own ring.
On the logarithmic clock, $x$ at $\log_2 x + 1$ of a turn, the floor of Order $n$ sits at
$\{n\alpha\}$: the circle of fifths. As $\log_2\theta_n = n\alpha - p_2(n)$ is linear in $n$ up to an
integer, the floors of $n$ and $n + 12$ lie on one line (a spiral on the clock), turning by
$\log_2(3^{12}/2^{19})$, the Pythagorean comma, per $12$ Orders, and those of $n$ and $n + 5$ on
another, turning by $-\log_2(256/243)$, the limma, per $5$ Orders; at Order $0$ the $12$ lines start
at the $12$ equal steps of the octave, the $5$ at its $5$ equal steps. The convergent denominators
$12, 53, 665, \dots$ of $\alpha$ are the steps of the families of lines that reach $x = 1$ at the
record Orders $5, 17, 29, 41$ (steps $12$), $94, 147, 200, 253, 306$ (steps $53$), $971, 1636$
(steps $665$).

On the linear clock (Figure~\ref{fig:octave}), $x$ at $2x - 1$ of a turn, the ratios of Order $n$
spread over an arc from the floor (their infimum) to the largest ratio, which is at the first start
of a class: the best of the Order (Section~\ref{sec:cycle}) (OEIS A402443). Its length is $2\rho^\ast/(2^L c^\ast)$ of a turn,
$c^\ast$ the best and $\rho^\ast$ its constant: short where $c^\ast$ is large.

\begin{figure}[ht]

\caption{The linear octave clock, Orders $0$ to $20$ (Order $n$ on the $n$-th ring from the
centre), $1 = 1/2$ at the top, clockwise. Red rings: the floors $3^n/2^{p_2(n)}$. Blue: the ratios

($n$, $n + 12$), dotted: of the limma ($n$, $n + 5$).}
\label{fig:octave}

Unrolled (Figure~\ref{fig:floordots}), the octave as the horizontal axis and the Order upward, the floors
alone show the records: $\theta_n > \theta_m$ for all $1 \le m < n$ exactly when $e_n < e_m$ for all
$1 \le m < n$, so the floors further right than every floor below them are at the upper record
Orders $1, 3, 5, 17, 29, \dots$, where $\binom{L}{n} = L\,N(n)$ (OEIS A400061 is $0$); the floors further
left than every floor below them are at the lower record Orders $1, 2, 7, 12, 53, 359, 665, \dots$.

On the $2$-adic clock, a start $x = \sum b_j 2^j$ at $\sum b_j 2^{-j-1}$ of a turn (its bits read from
the lowest), the class of a drop word is an arc of length $2^{-L}$; the arcs of all Orders are
disjoint and fill the circle up to measure $0$: Lemma~\ref{lem:mass}, $P(1) = 1$.

%------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\clearpage
\section{The drop zeta}
\label{sec:zeta}

\begin{definition}
For $s \in \C$,
\[
P(s) = \sum_{n \ge 0} N(n)\, 3^{n(s-1)} 2^{-p_2(n)s} = \sum_{n \ge 0} m_n \theta_n^{\,s-1},
\qquad Z(s) = \frac{1}{1 - P(s)}.
\]
\end{definition}

One term per drop word: the $2$-adic size $2^{-L}$ of its class times the slope of its branch to
the power $s - 1$. The first terms are $2^{-s}$ (the word $0$) and $3^{s-1}4^{-s}$ (the word $10$).
We call $P$ the drop prime function and $Z$ the drop zeta function, after the prime zeta function
$P(s) = \sum_p p^{-s}$ and $\zeta(s)$; the analogy is made precise in Section~\ref{sec:euler}.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:entire}
$P$ is entire, $P(1) = 1$, and $Z$ is meromorphic on $\C$ without zeros. At $s = 1$, $Z$ has a simple
pole with residue $1/\mu$, $\mu = -P'(1) = \sum_n m_n \lambda_n > 0$, $\lambda_n = \ln(2^{p_2(n)}/3^n)$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
With $\sigma = \Rea s$, $|m_n \theta_n^{\,s-1}| = m_n \theta_n^{\,\sigma-1} \le m_n 2^{\max(0, 1-\sigma)}$, as
$1/2 \le \theta_n < 1$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:mass} the series converges absolutely and uniformly on
compact sets, so $P$ is entire and $P(1) = 1$; $Z$ is the reciprocal of an entire function that is
not identically $1$ ($P(s) \to 0$ as $\sigma \to \infty$), so it is meromorphic without zeros. Its
poles are the zeros of $1 - P$. Near $s = 1$, $1 - P(s) = \mu(s-1) + O((s-1)^2)$ with
$\mu = -\sum m_n \ln\theta_n = \sum m_n \lambda_n$, finite as $0 < \lambda_n \le \ln 2$, and positive.
\end{proof}

The prime zeta function, in contrast, has the natural boundary $\Rea s = 0$.

\begin{remark}
The value $P(0) = \sum_n N(n)/3^n$ is the mean number of starts per drop value: the residue at
$s = 1$ of $\sum_{x} d(x)^{-s}$, $d(x)$ the drop value $T^L(x)$ of the start $x$.
\end{remark}

\begin{computation}\label{comp:values}
The values at small integers, as certified continued fractions (exact rational arithmetic, the
sum over the Orders up to $399$ exact and the tail bounded by $2^{\max(0, 1-s)}(1 - \sum_{n \le 399} m_n)$,
which is below $10^{-12}$):

\medskip
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}rll@{}}
\toprule
$s$ & $P(s)$ & $Z(s)$\\
\midrule
$-2$ & $[5; 2, 2, 2, 1, 4, 7, 8, 1, 1, 9, 1, \dots]$ & $[-1; 1, 3, 2, 2, 2, 1, 4, 7, 8, 1, 1, 9, 1, \dots]$\\
$-1$ & $[2; 1, 39, 1, 2, 1, 4, 6, \dots]$ & $[-1; 2, 39, 1, 2, 1, 4, 6, \dots]$\\
$0$ & $[1; 1, 2, 4, 2, 1, 4, 4, 1, 2, 6, 3, 2, \dots]$ & $[-2; 1, 1, 4, 2, 1, 4, 4, 1, 2, 6, 3, 2, \dots]$\\
$1$ & $1$ & pole, residue $1/\mu$\\
$2$ & $[0; 1, 1, 1, 1, 1, 2, 2, 7, 2, 7, 1, 5, 1, 3, 1, \dots]$ & $[2; 1, 1, 1, 2, 2, 7, 2, 7, 1, 5, 1, 3, 1, \dots]$\\
$3$ & $[0; 2, 2, 9, 3, 1, 4, 2, 1, 1, 7, \dots]$ & $[1; 1, 2, 9, 3, 1, 4, 2, 1, 1, 7, \dots]$\\

\end{center}
