\clearpage
\section{The Euler product}
\label{sec:euler}

As no drop word is a proper prefix of another, a concatenation of drop words factors into drop
words in only one way. For $\Rea s > 1$, $|P(s)| \le P(\Rea s) < 1$, and so
\[
Z(s) = \sum_{k \ge 0} P(s)^k = \exp\Bigl(\sum_{k \ge 1} \frac{P(s)^k}{k}\Bigr).
\]
The first sum runs over all concatenations of drop words, each weighted by the product of the terms
of its factors: the drop words play the primes, in a free (not commutative) monoid, and
$P = 1 - 1/Z$ takes the place of the M\"obius inversion that expresses the prime zeta function by
$\log\zeta(ks)$, $k \ge 1$.

The second sum is a dynamical zeta function. Let $D$ be the \emph{drop map}: on the class of a drop
word $w$, $D(x) = T^L(x) = (3^n x + \rho_w)/2^L$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:mass} it is defined on a subset
of $\Z_2$ of full measure, and it maps each class onto $\Z_2$, as $3^n$ is a $2$-adic unit. So $D$
is a full shift on the drop words: for each sequence $w_1, \dots, w_k$ of drop words, $D^k$ maps
the points that read them one after the other affinely onto $\Z_2$; the inverse branch is a
$2$-adic contraction, so it has exactly one fixed point, a fixed point of $D^k$. Weighting a point
by the term of its word,
\[
\sum_{x \in \mathrm{Fix}(D^k)} \prod_{j < k} t(D^j x) = P(s)^k, \qquad
Z(s) = \prod_{\gamma} \bigl(1 - w_\gamma(s)\bigr)^{-1},
\]
the product over the prime cycles $\gamma$ of $D$, $w_\gamma$ the product of the terms of the words
along $\gamma$ (after Artin and Mazur~\cite{ArtinMazur1965}, with weights).

The periodic points of $D$ are the rationals $x = \rho/(2^M - 3^N) \ge 0$, $\rho$, $N$ and $M$
those of the concatenated word, $2^M > 3^N$; so no negative integer is one ($-1$, a fixed point of
$T$, never drops). Two integers are: $0$ (the word $0$, the factor $(1 - 2^{-s})^{-1}$, the Euler
factor of $\zeta(s)$ at $2$) and $1$ (the word $10$, the factor $(1 - 3^{s-1}4^{-s})^{-1}$). A third
integer cycle would be a cycle of $T$ through a positive integer $x > 1$ that does not drop at the
end of its word: against the width identity of its Order (paper~2~\cite{Paper2}), which
paper~3~\cite{Paper3} verifies for every Order up to $64\,497\,106$, given its range check. So the integer factors
of the product are these two exactly when $T$ has no cycle on the positive integers besides
$\{1, 2\}$.

%------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\clearpage
\section{The poles}
\label{sec:poles}

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:band}
In each band $B_k = \{s : |\Ima s - 2\pi k/\ln 2| < \pi/\ln 2\}$, $k \in \Z$, the function $1 - P$
has exactly one zero $s_k$, and it is simple; $s_0 = 1$. The same holds with any $r_n$ in place of
$\theta_n$, $r_0 = 1/2$ and $1/2 < r_n < 1$ for $n \ge 1$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Compare $f = 1 - P$ with $g(s) = 1 - 2^{-s}$, which has exactly one zero in $B_k$, the simple zero
$2\pi i k/\ln 2$. Put $h = f - g = -\sum_{n \ge 1} m_n r_n^{\,s-1}$; then
$|h(s)| \le H(\sigma) := \sum_{n \ge 1} m_n r_n^{\,\sigma-1}$, and $\sum_{n \ge 1} m_n = 1/2$. On the edges
$\Ima s = (2k \pm 1)\pi/\ln 2$ of the band, $2^{-s} = -2^{-\sigma}$, so $|g| = 1 + 2^{-\sigma}$, while
$H(\sigma) \le 1/2$ for $\sigma \ge 1$ and $H(\sigma) < \frac12 2^{1-\sigma} = 2^{-\sigma}$ for $\sigma < 1$
(as $r_n > 1/2$). As $\sigma \to +\infty$, $H \to 0$ and $|g| \to 1$; as $\sigma \to -\infty$,
$H(\sigma)/2^{-\sigma} = 2\sum_{n \ge 1} m_n (2r_n)^{\sigma - 1} \to 0$ by dominated convergence, while
$|g| \ge 2^{-\sigma} - 1$. So $|h| < |g|$ on the boundary of the rectangle
$\{|\Rea s| \le A\} \cap B_k$ for all large $A$, and by Rouch\'e's theorem $f$ and $g$ have the same
number of zeros inside, counted with multiplicity: one.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:line}
No zero of $1 - P$ but $s = 1$ has $\Rea s \ge 1$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For $\sigma \ge 1$, $|P(s)| \le \sum m_n \theta_n^{\,\sigma-1} \le 1$, with equality in the second
step only if $\sigma = 1$, and then $P(s) = 1$ only if all terms $m_n \theta_n^{\,it}$, $s = 1 + it$,
are positive. For $n = 0$ and $n = 1$ this says $t\ln 2 \in 2\pi\Z$ and $t \ln(4/3) \in 2\pi\Z$; as
$\ln(4/3)/\ln 2 = 2 - \alpha$ is irrational, $t = 0$.
\end{proof}

This is the argument MathWorld~\cite{MathWorldPZ} gives for the boundary of the zero-free half-plane
of the prime zeta function (two terms whose logarithms have an irrational ratio), here on the line
$\Rea s = 1$ itself: the prime zeta function is zero-free for $\Rea s \ge \sigma_0$ with a bound
$\sigma_0 > 1$~\cite{Belovas2025}, sharp~\cite{Caceres2026}; $Z$ has its pole at $s = 1$ and no other
pole on the line.

\subsection*{One curve}

As $\theta_n^{\,2\pi i k/\ln 2} = e^{2\pi i k(n\alpha - p_2(n))} = e^{2\pi i n k \alpha}$,
\[
1 - P\Bigl(w + \frac{2\pi i k}{\ln 2}\Bigr) = F_\beta(w) := 1 - \sum_{n \ge 0} m_n \theta_n^{\,w-1} e^{2\pi i n \beta},
\qquad \beta = k\alpha \bmod 1.
\]
The proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:band} applies to $F_\beta$ for every real $\beta$ (only the moduli of the
coefficients enter): $F_\beta$ has exactly one zero $z(\beta)$ with $|\Ima z(\beta)| < \pi/\ln 2$, and it
is simple. So $s_k = 2\pi i k/\ln 2 + z(k\alpha \bmod 1)$: the poles lie on one closed curve, sampled
by the rotation by $\alpha$. By the implicit function theorem $z$ is real analytic on
$\mathbb{R}/\Z$; $z(0) = 1$, $z(-\beta) = \overline{z(\beta)}$, so $z(1/2)$ is real.

\begin{theorem}[The bend]\label{thm:bend}
$\Rea z(\beta) = 1 - \dfrac{2\pi^2 V}{\mu}\,\beta^2 + O(\beta^4)$, with
$V = \sum_n m_n \bigl(n - \tfrac{\nu}{\mu}\lambda_n\bigr)^2 > 0$ and $\nu = \sum_n n\, m_n$. For
$\beta \not\equiv 0$, $\Rea z(\beta) < 1$. Hence there are constants $c_2 \ge c_1 > 0$ with
$c_1\|k\alpha\|^2 \le 1 - \Rea s_k \le c_2\|k\alpha\|^2$ for all $k \ne 0$, and the closure of
$\{\Rea s_k : k \ge 1\}$ is $[\min \Rea z, 1]$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The masses are a probability distribution on the Orders (Lemma~\ref{lem:mass}); write $E$ for its
mean. With $X = -(z-1)\lambda_n + 2\pi i \beta n$, $z = z(\beta)$ solves $E[e^X] = 1$, that is
$\log E[e^X] = 0$. Expand in cumulants with $z - 1 = a\beta + b\beta^2 + O(\beta^3)$: the first order
gives $-a\mu + 2\pi i\nu = 0$, so $a = 2\pi i\nu/\mu$; the second gives
$-b\mu + \frac12\mathrm{Var}(-a\lambda_n + 2\pi i n) = 0$, and $-a\lambda_n + 2\pi i n =
2\pi i(n - \frac{\nu}{\mu}\lambda_n)$, a variable of mean $0$, so $b = -2\pi^2 V/\mu$. The third order
terms are $O(\beta^3)$, and $\Rea z$ is even in $\beta$ as $z(-\beta) = \overline{z(\beta)}$. $V > 0$ as
$n - \frac{\nu}{\mu}\lambda_n$ takes different values at the Orders $0$ and $1$ ($\lambda_1 < \lambda_0$,
and $\nu, \mu > 0$). If $\Rea z(\beta) \ge 1$, the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:line}
applied to $F_\beta$ gives, from the Orders $0$ and $1$, $\Ima z(\beta) = 0$ and $e^{2\pi i\beta} = 1$.
So $(1 - \Rea z(\beta))/\|\beta\|^2$ is continuous and positive on $\mathbb{R}/\Z$ minus $0$, with the
positive limit $2\pi^2V/\mu$ at $0$: it lies between two positive constants. The closure statement
follows as $k\alpha \bmod 1$ is dense and $\Rea z$ is continuous.
\end{proof}

By Rhin's bound~\cite{Rhin1987}, $|k\ln 3 - j\ln 2| \ge \max(j, k)^{-133/10}$ for large $\max(j,k)$, so
$1 - \Rea s_k$ is at least a constant times $k^{-133/5}$; and it is of the order $1/q^2$ at the
convergent denominators $q$ of $\alpha$.

\begin{computation}[The fall]\label{comp:fall}
$\Rea z$ falls strictly on $[0, 1/2]$. Certified by disc arithmetic over $2004$ middle cells of $\beta$ and two end cells: per
cell a Rouch\'e disc holding $z(\beta)$ for the whole cell, then enclosures of the derivatives of
$F_\beta(w)$ over disc and cell, with the Orders beyond $1000$ bounded by $m_n \le \binom{p_2(n)}{n}2^{-p_2(n)}$;
$\Rea z' < 0$ on the middle cells, and $\Rea z'' < 0$ on $[0, 1/256]$, $\Rea z'' > 0$ on
$[1/2 - 1/65536, 1/2]$, where $\Rea z' = 0$ at both ends by symmetry. Hence $\min\Rea z = z(1/2) = [-1; 1, 1, 1, 2, 1, 7, 1, 5, 2, 2, 3, 27, 2, 1, 1,
6, 7, 2, 1, 86, 6, 1, \dots]$ (23 terms, certified by a sign change); $\Rea s_j > \Rea s_k$ exactly
when $\|j\alpha\| < \|k\alpha\|$; and the poles nearest to $\Rea s = 1$ so far come at
$k = 1, 2, 5, 12, 41, 53, 306, 665, 15601, \dots$, the convergent denominators of
$\alpha$ (OEIS A005664), at which $\|k\alpha\|$ has its records. The first poles:

\medskip
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}rll@{}}
\toprule
$k$ & $\Rea s_k$ & $\Ima s_k$\\
\midrule
$1$ & $[-1; 1, 1, 1, 16, 1, 2, 6, \dots]$ & $[8; 1, 10, 1, 1, 2, 3, \dots]$\\
$2$ & $[0; 15, 1, 2, 8, 1, 3, 4, \dots]$ & $[18; 1, 1, 1, 2, 1, 1, 2, \dots]$\\
$3$ & $[-1; 1, 7, 79, 2, 1, 1, 2, \dots]$ & $[26; 1, 3, 2, 2, 6, 2, 6, \dots]$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\end{computation}

\clearpage
\section{The mass per Order}
\label{sec:mass}

Fix $n \ge 0$ and $L = p_2(n)$, and let $W(n)$ be the $\binom{L}{n}$ words of length $L$ with $n$ ones.
Every word of $W(n)$ has level $3^n/2^L < 1$. The \emph{rotation} of a word $u$ of length $L$ by
$r \le L$ is $u_r = u_{[r, L)}\,u_{[0, r)}$.

\begin{definition}
A \emph{ladder word} is a word whose level is below the level of each of its proper prefixes (the
empty one included). Let $\Lad(n) \subseteq W(n)$ be the ladder words in $W(n)$.
\end{definition}

A drop word is a ladder word, and $\Drop(n) \subseteq \Lad(n)$. The levels of a rotation are
$\lv(u_r, t) = \lv(u, r+t)/\lv(u, r)$ for $t \le L - r$ and
$\lv(u_r, t) = \lv(u, L)\lv(u, t - L + r)/\lv(u, r)$ for $t > L - r$.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:ladrot}
(i) $u_r$ is a ladder word iff $\lv(u, r) < \lv(u, j)$ for all $j < r$ and
$\lv(u, r)\lv(u, L) < \lv(u, s)$ for all $r \le s < L$.
(ii) If $\lv(u, L) < 1$, the rotation at the lowest level, $r$ the $i < L$ with the least
$\lv(u, i)$, is a ladder word.
(iii) For a ladder word $v$ and $0 < r < L$, $v_r$ is a ladder word iff $\lv(v, r) < \lv(v, j)$ for all
$j < r$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(i) is the formula for the levels of $u_r$. (ii): the levels are distinct, and
$\lv(u, r)\lv(u, L) < \lv(u, r) \le \lv(u, s)$. (iii): the condition with $j = 0$ gives
$\lv(v, r) < 1$, and then $\lv(v, r)\lv(v, L) < \lv(v, L) < \lv(v, s)$ for every $s < L$.
\end{proof}

For a ladder word $v$ of length $L$ let
\[
\tau(v) = \{\, r < L : v_s \text{ is not a ladder word for } L - r \le s < L \,\}.
\]

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:bij}
Every $u \in W(n)$ is $v_{L-r}$ for exactly one pair $(v, r)$ with $v \in \Lad(n)$ and
$r \in \tau(v)$. Hence $\binom{L}{n} = \sum_{v \in \Lad(n)} |\tau(v)|$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For $v$ ladder and $r \in \tau(v)$, $u = v_{L-r}$ has $u_r = v$, a ladder word, and $u_s = v_{L - r + s}$
for $s < r$, not a ladder word: so $r$ is the first ladder rotation of $u$, and $v = u_r$. This
gives uniqueness. Conversely, $u$ has a ladder rotation by Lemma~\ref{lem:ladrot}(ii); let $r$ be the
first. Then $v = u_r \in \Lad(n)$, $u = v_{L-r}$, and for $L - r \le s < L$, $v_s = u_{r + s - L}$
with $r + s - L < r$, not a ladder word; so $r \in \tau(v)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:split}
(i) For $d \in \Drop(n)$, $\tau(d) = \{0, \dots, L - 1\}$.
(ii) Let $1 \le m < n$ with $p_2(m) + p_2(n-m) = p_2(n)$. For $d \in \Drop(m)$ and $w \in \Lad(n-m)$,
$d\,w \in \Lad(n) \setminus \Drop(n)$ and $\tau(dw) = \tau(w)$.
(iii) Every $v \in \Lad(n) \setminus \Drop(n)$ is $d\,w$ as in (ii) for exactly one $m$, $d$, $w$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(i) By Lemma~\ref{lem:ladrot}(iii) with $j = 0$, $d_s$ ($0 < s < L$) would need $\lv(d, s) < 1$.

(ii) The levels of $dw$ are $\lv(d, i) \ge 1$ for $i < |d|$ and $\lv(d, |d|)\lv(w, j)$ after that, and
$\lv(dw, L) = \lv(d, |d|)\lv(w, |w|) < 1$: so $dw$ is a ladder word, in $W(n)$ by the lengths, and not
a drop word, as its proper prefix $d$ has level below $1$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:ladrot}(iii), the rotation
of $dw$ by $|d|$ is a ladder word, and for $0 < j < |w|$ the rotation by $|d| + j$ is one iff $w_j$
is one (the levels before $|d|$ are at least $1$, above $\lv(d, |d|)\lv(w, j)$ when
$\lv(w, j) < 1$). So for $r < L$, $r \in \tau(dw)$ iff $r < |w|$ and $r \in \tau(w)$.

(iii) Let $g$ be the least $i \ge 1$ with $\lv(v, i) < 1$; $g < L$ as $v$ is not a drop word. The
first $g$ letters form a drop word $d$, of some Order $m$ and length $g = p_2(m)$; the rest $w$ is a
ladder word, as $\lv(v, g + j) = \lv(d, g)\lv(w, j)$. Its Order is $n - m$ and its length $L - g$, with
$3^{n-m} < 2^{L-g}$ (as $\lv(w, L-g) < 1$) and $2^{L-1} < 3^n = 3^m 3^{n-m} < 2^g 3^{n-m}$: so
$L - g = p_2(n - m)$. Then $m \ge 1$ (else $p_2(n) = 1 + p_2(n)$) and $m < n$ (else $g = L$). The
split is unique, as no drop word is a proper prefix of another.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:mass}
For every $n \ge 0$, with $L = p_2(n)$,
\[
\binom{L}{n} = L\,N(n) + \sum_{m \in S(n)} N(m)\binom{p_2(n-m)}{n-m},
\]
with $S(n) = \{1 \le m < n : p_2(m) + p_2(n-m) = p_2(n)\}$; and for $1 \le m < n$: $m \in S(n)$ iff $2^{p_2(m)}3^n < 2^{p_2(n)}3^m$ iff $e_m < e_n$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
By Lemmas~\ref{lem:bij} and~\ref{lem:split},
\[
\binom{L}{n} = \sum_{d \in \Drop(n)} L
+ \sum_{m \in S(n)} \sum_{d \in \Drop(m)} \sum_{w \in \Lad(n-m)} |\tau(w)|,
\]
and the inner sum is
$\binom{p_2(n-m)}{n-m}$ by Lemma~\ref{lem:bij} at $n - m$. For the condition: put $a = p_2(m)$,
$b = p_2(n-m)$. From $3^n = 3^m 3^{n-m} < 2^{a+b}$ and $2^{a-1}2^{b-1} \le 3^n$, $p_2(n)$ is $a + b$ or
$a + b - 1$. Dividing by $3^m$, the inequality reads $2^a 3^{n-m} < 2^{p_2(n)}$, which holds for
$p_2(n) = a + b$ and fails for $p_2(n) = a + b - 1$, as $2^{b-1} \le 3^{n-m}$. Taking $\log_2$ it is
$e_m < e_n$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Winkler~\cite{Winkler2026}]
$L\,N(n) \le \binom{L}{n}$, with equality iff $S(n)$ is empty, that is iff $e_n < e_m$ for all
$1 \le m < n$: $n = 1, 3, 5, 17, 29, 41, 94, 147, 200, 253, 306, 971, 1636, \dots$, the denominators
of the upper semiconvergents of $\alpha$ (the upper record Orders of paper~2).
\end{corollary}

The difference $\binom{L}{n} - L\,N(n)$, the number of words of $W(n)$ none of whose rotations is a
drop word, is $0, 2, 0, 14, 0, 90, 432, 182, 2410, 392, 14526, \dots$ from $n = 1$
(OEIS A400061). Theorem~\ref{thm:mass} computes $N(1), N(2), \dots$ from binomial
coefficients alone, in time quadratic in the Orders.

\begin{remark}\label{rem:massex}
The identity can also be read off the cycle identity $\binom{L}{n} = L\sum_{k \ge 1} C_k(n)/k$, $C_k(n)$
the number of sequences of $k$ drop words of total Order $n$ and length $L$: the rotations of a word
at the records of its cyclic walk $W_i = i - a_i\alpha$ (Feller~\cite{Feller2}). The two sides of
Theorem~\ref{thm:mass} do not count the same words: for $n = 2$ the words with no drop rotation are
$1010$ and $0101$, those of the sum ($10$, then one $1$ in two letters) $1010$ and $1001$.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:jumps}
With $p = 1/\alpha$, $\sum_{m \ge 1} N(m)\,p^m(1-p)^{p_2(m)-m} = p$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Read a word with independent letters, a one with probability $p$. Put $W_i = i - a_i\alpha$, $a_i$
the ones among the first $i$ letters; a zero adds $1$, a one $1 - \alpha$, and the mean step is
$(1 - p) + p(1 - \alpha) = 0$. A random walk with mean-zero steps that are not all $0$ has
$\limsup W_i = +\infty$ almost surely (Feller~\cite{Feller2}), so it passes above $0$ almost surely,
and the first time it does is the end of a drop word ($W_i > 0$ means level below $1$). These are
disjoint events, of probability $N(m)p^m(1-p)^{p_2(m)-m}$ for the drop words of Order $m$. So the
sum over $m \ge 0$ is $1$, and the word $0$ gives $1 - p$.
\end{proof}

Let $G(e) = 1 - \sum_{m \ge 1,\, e_m < e} N(m)\,p^m(1-p)^{p_2(m)-m}$, a staircase from $1$ (as $e \to 0$)
down to $1 - p$ (as $e \to 1$, by Theorem~\ref{thm:jumps}).

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:limit}
As $n \to \infty$, with $L = p_2(n)$, $L\,N(n)/\binom{L}{n} - G(e_n) \to 0$. Hence
$N(n)/3^n \sim G(e_n)\,(\alpha/(\alpha-1))^{e_n}\,\kappa^n/(\sqrt{2\pi\alpha(\alpha-1)}\,n^{3/2})$ with
$\kappa = \alpha^\alpha/(3(\alpha-1)^{\alpha-1})$ ($3\kappa$ is Winkler's $B_3$~\cite{Winkler2026}).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Read the words of $W(n)$ with equal weights. As $W_0 = 0$ and $W_L = e_n > 0$, every word passes above
$0$, and the first time it does is the end of a drop word, of some Order $m \le n$ and length
$p_2(m)$ (the word $0$ for $m = 0$). So $P_n(m) = N(m)\binom{L - p_2(m)}{n - m}/\binom{L}{n}$, $0 \le m \le n$,
add up to $1$. For fixed $m$, $P_n(m)$ is a product of $p_2(m)$ factors $(n - i)/(L - j)$ and
$(L - n - i)/(L - j)$, so $P_n(m) \to P(m) = N(m)p^m(1-p)^{p_2(m)-m}$ as $n/L \to p$; and $\sum_{m \ge 0}
P(m) = 1$ by Theorem~\ref{thm:jumps}. Then $\sum_m |P_n(m) - P(m)| = 2\sum_m (P(m) - P_n(m))^+ \to 0$
by dominated convergence, as $(P(m) - P_n(m))^+ \le P(m)$ (Scheff\'e's argument). By
Theorem~\ref{thm:mass}, $1 - L\,N(n)/\binom{L}{n} = \sum_{m \in S(n)} P_n(m)$, the sum over the
$1 \le m < n$ with $e_m < e_n$, while $1 - G(e_n)$ is the sum of $P(m)$ over all $m \ge 1$ with $e_m < e_n$;
their difference is at most $\sum_m |P_n(m) - P(m)|$. For the second part, Stirling's formula with
$L = \alpha n + e_n$ gives $\binom{L}{n}/L \sim (3\kappa)^n(\alpha/(\alpha-1))^{e_n}/(\sqrt{2\pi\alpha(\alpha-1)}\,n^{3/2})$,
uniformly as $0 < e_n < 1$, and $G(e_n) \ge 1 - p > 0$.
\end{proof}

So $1 - L\,N(n)/\binom{L}{n}$, the share of the words with no drop rotation (OEIS A400061 over
$\binom{L}{n}$), is the chance that the first drop of a word of $W(n)$ has an Order $m$ with
$e_m < e_n$, and $1 - G(e_n)$ the same chance for a word with independent letters.

\begin{theorem}[Winkler, private communication]\label{thm:rate}
$L\,N(n)/\binom{L}{n} - G(e_n) = O(n^{-1/2})$, with $L = p_2(n)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Put $w(m) = N(m)p^m(1-p)^{p_2(m)-m}$ and $Q(j) = \binom{p_2(j)}{j}p^j(1-p)^{p_2(j)-j}$. For
$m \in S(n)$, $L - p_2(m) = p_2(n - m)$, and the powers of $p$ and $1 - p$ cancel:
$P_n(m) = w(m)\,Q(n-m)/Q(n)$. So, by the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:limit},
$G(e_n) - L\,N(n)/\binom{L}{n} = \sum_{m \in S(n)} w(m)\,(Q(n-m)/Q(n) - 1) - \sum_{m \ge n,\, e_m < e_n} w(m)$.
As $p\,p_2(j) - j = p\,e_j$ is bounded, Stirling's formula gives $Q(j) = (2\pi(1-p)j)^{-1/2}(1 + O(1/j))$,
and Winkler's bound $N(m) \le \binom{p_2(m)}{m}/p_2(m)$~\cite{Winkler2026} gives
$w(m) \le Q(m)/p_2(m) = O(m^{-3/2})$. For $m \le n/2$, $Q(n-m)/Q(n) = 1 + O(m/n)$, and these terms add
up to $O(n^{-1}\sum_{m \le n/2} m^{-1/2}) = O(n^{-1/2})$. For $n/2 < m < n$, $w(m)\,Q(n-m)/Q(n) =
O(n^{-1}(n-m)^{-1/2})$, again $O(n^{-1/2})$ in all; and $\sum_{m > n/2} w(m) = O(n^{-1/2})$.
\end{proof}

As $e_n$ is dense in $(0, 1)$ and $G$ runs from $1$ down to $1 - p$, the share of the words with no
drop rotation has $\liminf 0$ and $\limsup p$; it is $0$ exactly at the upper record Orders.

\begin{observation}\label{obs:massdiff}
The difference in Theorem~\ref{thm:limit} (Figure~\ref{fig:mass}) stays below $1/70$ for the Orders $100$ to $399$ (the jumps of
the Orders up to $5000$ computed, the rest bounded by Theorem~\ref{thm:jumps}; floating point).
\end{observation}

\begin{corollary}\label{cor:massrate}
With $\kappa$ as in Theorem~\ref{thm:limit}, the mass of Order $n$ is
\[
m_n = G(e_n)\Bigl(\frac{\alpha}{2(\alpha-1)}\Bigr)^{e_n}
\frac{\kappa^n}{\sqrt{2\pi\alpha(\alpha-1)}\,n^{3/2}}\bigl(1 + O(n^{-1/2})\bigr).
\]
So $\log_2 m_n = n\log_2\kappa - \frac32\log_2 n + F(e_n) + O(n^{-1/2})$, with
$F(e) = \log_2 G(e) + e\log_2\frac{\alpha}{2(\alpha-1)} - \log_2\sqrt{2\pi\alpha(\alpha-1)}$, and
$F(1^-) = F(0^+) - 1$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
$m_n = \theta_n N(n)/3^n$ with $\theta_n = 2^{-e_n}$. By Theorem~\ref{thm:rate} and $G(e_n) \ge 1 - p > 0$,
$L\,N(n)/\binom{L}{n} = G(e_n)(1 + O(n^{-1/2}))$, and Stirling's formula in the proof of
Theorem~\ref{thm:limit} holds with a factor $1 + O(1/n)$, uniformly as $0 < e_n < 1$. For the last
part: $G(0^+) = 1$, $G(1^-) = 1 - p$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:jumps}) and $\alpha/(\alpha - 1) = 1/(1 - p)$.
\end{proof}

The tilt in $F$ is exact. Let $w(n) = N(n)\,p^n(1-p)^{p_2(n)-n}$, the jump of $G$ at $e_n$: the chance that
a word with independent letters, a one with chance $p$, has its first drop at Order $n$. Then
$1 - G(e)$ is the chance that this first drop has its floor above $2^{-e}$ (Corollary~\ref{thm:stair}).

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:tilt}
For $n \ge 1$, with $L = p_2(n)$,
\[
w(n) = \frac{N(n)\,(1-p)^{e_n}}{(3\kappa)^n}, \qquad
m_n = \Bigl(\frac{1}{2p}\Bigr)^{n}\Bigl(\frac{1}{2(1-p)}\Bigr)^{L-n} w(n)
    = \kappa^n\Bigl(\frac{\alpha}{2(\alpha-1)}\Bigr)^{e_n} w(n).
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
$m_n = N(n)/2^L$ and $w(n) = N(n)p^n(1-p)^{L-n}$ give the middle form. With $L = \alpha n + e_n$,
$p^n(1-p)^{L-n} = \bigl(p\,(1-p)^{\alpha-1}\bigr)^n(1-p)^{e_n}$, and
$p\,(1-p)^{\alpha-1} = (\alpha-1)^{\alpha-1}/\alpha^\alpha = 1/(3\kappa)$. Then
$m_n = N(n)\,3^{-n}2^{-e_n}$ (as $2^L = 3^n 2^{e_n}$), and $1/(2(1-p)) = \alpha/(2(\alpha-1))$.
\end{proof}

So the masses are the first-drop chances of the letters with chance $p$, weighed again with chance
$1/2$ per letter; the factor $(\alpha/(2(\alpha-1)))^{e_n}$ of Corollary~\ref{cor:massrate} is that
change of weights, and the corollary is Theorem~\ref{thm:rate} read as
$w(n) = G(e_n)\binom{L}{n}p^n(1-p)^{L-n}/L\,\bigl(1 + O(n^{-1/2})\bigr)$. In the series
$D(c, y) = \sum_n N(n)\,c^n y^{e_n}$, the drop zeta $P(s)$ lies on $c = 1/3$ ($y = 2^{-s}$), the
first-drop chances on $c = 1/(3\kappa)$, where the series stops converging.

In a $\log_2$ plot of the masses against the Order, they follow the curve
$n\log_2\kappa - \frac32\log_2 n$, which bends upward (so no straight line is a tight bound),
shifted by $F(e_n)$: the staircase $\log_2 G$ tilted by a linear term in $e_n$, over a total
height of $1$ from $e_n$ near $0$ to $e_n$ near $1$. The band $N(n)/(2\cdot 3^n) < m_n < N(n)/3^n$,
by contrast, is just $1/2 < \theta_n < 1$.

\caption{The mass per Order: $L\,N(n)/\binom{L}{n}$ against $e_n$ for the Orders $1$ to $399$, and the
staircase $G$ of Theorem~\ref{thm:limit} (its jumps to Order $5000$), from $1$ down to $1 - p$

\label{fig:mass}
\end{figure}

\subsection*{Floors and counts}
\label{sec:counts}

Fix $n \ge 1$, $L = p_2(n)$, and let $W(n)$ be the $\binom{L}{n}$ words of length $L$ with $n$ ones. The
\emph{rotation} of $u = u_0 \cdots u_{L-1}$ by $r$ is $u_r \cdots u_{L-1} u_0 \cdots u_{r-1}$.

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:counts}
\[
\binom{L}{n} = L\,N(n) + \sum_{\substack{1 \le m < n\\ \theta_m > \theta_n}} N(m)\binom{p_2(n-m)}{n-m}.
\]
The words of $W(n)$ none of whose rotations is a drop word number the sum (OEIS A400061), and they are
none iff $n$ is an upper record Order.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Theorem~\ref{thm:mass} is the identity with the sum over the
$1 \le m < n$ with $p_2(m) + p_2(n-m) = p_2(n)$, that is with $\theta_m > \theta_n$ by
Lemma~\ref{lem:p2split}. A drop word of Order $n$ has $L$ distinct rotations, and no two drop words are
rotations of each other, as no rotation of a drop word by $0 < s < L$ is a drop
word (Lemma~\ref{lem:split}\,(i)); so $L\,N(n)$ words of $W(n)$ are rotations of drop words, and the rest number the sum. The sum
is empty iff $\theta_n > \theta_m$ for all $1 \le m < n$, and a nonempty sum is positive, as
$N(m) \ge 1$.
\end{proof}

So the words with no drop rotation are counted by the floors above $\theta_n$: one term for each Order
$m < n$ whose dot in Figure~\ref{fig:floordots} lies to the right of the dot of $n$. The equality case,
$L\,N(n) = \binom{L}{n}$ exactly at the upper record Orders, is Winkler's~\cite{Winkler2026}, by the cycle
lemma. The terms do not count the words with no drop rotation one by one: for $n = 2$ these are
$1010$ and $0101$, while the term of $m = 1$ counts $10$ followed by a word of $W(1)$, $1010$ and
$1001$ (Remark~\ref{rem:massex}).

\subsection*{The staircase}
\label{sec:stair}

Read the words of $W(n)$ with equal weights. Every word passes below level $1$ before its end, and
the first time it does ends a drop word, of some Order $m \le n$; the chance that this is Order $m$ is
\[
P_n(m) = N(m)\binom{L - p_2(m)}{n - m}\Big/\binom{L}{n},
\]
and these add up to $1$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:limit}). By Theorem~\ref{thm:counts}, the share of the words
with no drop rotation is
\[
1 - \frac{L\,N(n)}{\binom{L}{n}} = \sum_{\substack{1 \le m < n\\ \theta_m > \theta_n}} P_n(m):
\]
the chance that the first drop of a word of $W(n)$ has its floor above $\theta_n$. For a word with
independent letters, a one with probability $p = 1/\alpha$, the first drop is at Order $m$ with chance
$N(m)p^m(1-p)^{p_2(m)-m}$, and these add up to $1$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:jumps}).

\begin{corollary}\label{thm:stair}
With $G(e) = 1 - \sum_{m \ge 1,\, e_m < e} N(m)\,p^m(1-p)^{p_2(m)-m}$, the share of the words of $W(n)$
with no drop rotation is $1 - G(e_n) + O(n^{-1/2})$: up to $O(n^{-1/2})$, the chance that the first drop
of a word with independent letters has its floor above $\theta_n$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Theorems~\ref{thm:limit} and~\ref{thm:rate}, as $e_m < e_n$ exactly when $\theta_m > \theta_n$.
\end{proof}

So the share of the words with no drop rotation is, in the limit, a staircase in the position of the
floor: $1 - G$ jumps at every floor $\theta_m$, by the chance of a first drop at Order $m$, from $0$ at
the top of the octave down to $p$ at its bottom. The difference stays below $1/70$ for the Orders $100$ to
$399$ (Observation~\ref{obs:massdiff} and Figure~\ref{fig:mass}). As the floors fill the octave, the share has
$\liminf 0$ (at the upper record Orders it is $0$) and $\limsup p$.

%------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\clearpage
\section{Compared with \texorpdfstring{$\zeta$}{zeta}}
\label{sec:compare}

\subsection*{The explicit formula}

Riemann's explicit formula turns the zeros of $\zeta$ into the harmonics of the prime count. Here
the poles of $Z$ play the zeros, and the count is the renewal count of the drop map. For a
$2$-adic start read by the Haar measure, let $S_k$ be the sum of the $\lambda_n = -\ln\theta_n$
of its first $k$ drop words, and
\[
U(u) = \sum_{k \ge 0} \Pr(S_k \le u) = \sum_{a\ln 2 - b\ln 3 \le u} C(a, b)\, 2^{-a},
\]
$C(a, b)$ the number of concatenations of drop words with $a$ letters, $b$ of them ones (including
the empty one). So $U(u)$ is the mean number of drops whose slopes multiply to at least $e^{-u}$, and
$\int_{[0,\infty)} e^{-tu}\,dU(u) = \sum_k P(1+t)^k = Z(1+t)$ for $\Rea t > 0$. $U$ jumps at a dense
set of points; its integral $U_1(u) = \int_0^u U(v)\,dv$ is continuous, and has an explicit formula.
With $M_j = \sum_n m_n \lambda_n^{\,j}$ (so $M_1 = \mu$), $c_1 = M_2/(2\mu)$ and
$c_2 = c_1^2 - M_3/(6\mu)$:

\begin{theorem}[The explicit formula]\label{thm:explicit}
For $u \ge 0$,
\[
U_1(u) = \frac{u^2/2 + c_1 u + c_2}{\mu} \;+\; \lim_{K \to \infty} \sum_{0 < |k| \le K}
\frac{e^{(s_k - 1)u}}{-P'(s_k)\,(s_k - 1)^2}.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
$U_1$ is continuous with $\int_0^\infty e^{-tu}U_1(u)\,du = Z(1+t)/t^2$, and on $\Rea s = c > 1$,
$|Z(s)| \le 1/(1 - P(c))$, so the inversion integral
$U_1(u) = \frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{c - i\infty}^{c + i\infty} Z(s)\, e^{(s-1)u} (s-1)^{-2}\, ds$ converges
absolutely. Close it by the rectangle with left edge $\Rea s = -A$ and horizontal edges
$\Ima s = \pm(2K+1)\pi/\ln 2$. On the horizontal edges, by the estimates in the proof of
Theorem~\ref{thm:band}, $|1 - P(s)| \ge 1 + 2^{-\sigma} - H(\sigma) \ge 1/2$, so $|Z| \le 2$ and these
edges contribute $O(K^{-2})$ for $u \ge 0$. On the left edge, $|1 - P(s)| \ge 2^{A} - 1 - H(-A)$ and
$H(-A)/2^{A} \to 0$, so $|Z(s)\,e^{(s-1)u}| = O(2^{-A})$ and that edge vanishes as $A \to \infty$. Inside
lie the poles $s = 1$ and $s_k$, $0 < |k| \le K$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:band}). At $s_k$, a simple zero of
$1 - P$, the residue is $e^{(s_k-1)u}/(-P'(s_k)(s_k-1)^2)$. At $s = 1$, write $t = s - 1$:
$1 - P(1 + t) = \mu t - \frac{M_2}{2}t^2 + \frac{M_3}{6}t^3 - \dots$, so
$Z(1+t) = (1 + c_1 t + c_2 t^2 + \dots)/(\mu t)$, and the residue of $Z(1+t)e^{tu}/t^2$ is
$(u^2/2 + c_1 u + c_2)/\mu$.
\end{proof}

So $U_1$ is a parabola plus one harmonic per pole: the $k$-th oscillates with frequency
$\Ima s_k/(2\pi)$ and dies like $e^{-(1 - \Rea s_k)u}$, and by Theorem~\ref{thm:bend} the slowest ones
are those at the convergent denominators $k$ of $\alpha$. At $u = j\ln 2$ the frequency $k/\ln 2$
of the band is invisible ($e^{2\pi i k j} = 1$), and only $\Ima z(k\alpha \bmod 1)$ is seen. (The
renewal theorem, $U(u) = u/\mu + M_2/(2\mu^2) + o(1)$~\cite{Feller2}, is the main term.)

\begin{computation}\label{comp:explicit}
The words counted by $C(a, b)$, read backwards, are the words whose nonempty prefixes all have level
below $1$ (a concatenation of drop words ends strictly below all its earlier levels), so
$C(a, b)$ follows from the ballot recursion $C(a, b) = C(a-1, b) + C(a-1, b-1)$ restricted to
$3^b < 2^a$. With $C$ exact for $a \le 1600$ and the poles $s_k$, $|k| \le 3000$, the two sides of
Theorem~\ref{thm:explicit} at $u = j\ln 2$, $0 \le j \le 40$, agree to within $2^{-16}$ at $j = 0$
(the poles beyond $3000$) and to within $2^{-40}$ from $j = 20$ on. The remainder after the parabola
changes sign between $j = 5$ and $6$, $21$ and $22$, $36$ and $37$: the harmonic of $s_{12}$, the
pole nearest to $\Rea s = 1$ for $|k| < 41$, beats with period about $30$ in $j$; with
$s_{41}$ and $s_{53}$ it carries most of the remainder at $j = 40$.
\end{computation}

\subsection*{The zeros of $P$}

The prime zeta function has its zeros to the left of a zero-free half-plane $\Rea s \ge \sigma_0 > 1$
(MathWorld~\cite{MathWorldPZ}). The drop prime function $P$ has them on the other side.

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:pzeros}
$P$ has no zeros with $\Rea s \le 1$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The term of Order $0$ is $2^{-s}$, of modulus $2^{-\sigma}$, and the other terms have moduli summing
to $H(\sigma) = \sum_{n \ge 1} m_n \theta_n^{\,\sigma-1}$. For $\sigma < 1$, $H(\sigma) < 2^{-\sigma}$
(proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:band}), so $P(s) \ne 0$. For $\sigma = 1$, $H(1) = 1/2 = 2^{-1}$, and
$P(1 + it) = 0$ forces all terms of Order $n \ge 1$ to have one argument, opposite to that of
$2^{-1-it}$. Orders $1$ and $2$ ($\theta_2 = \theta_1^2$) give $t\ln\theta_1 \in 2\pi\Z$, and Order $3$
($\theta_3 = 2\theta_1\theta_2$) then gives $t\ln 2 \in 2\pi\Z$; so the terms of Orders $0$ and $1$ are
both positive, not opposite.
\end{proof}

To the right, the terms trade places: as $\sigma$ grows, the term with the largest modulus
$m_n\theta_n^{\,\sigma-1}$ moves to Orders with $\theta_n$ closer to $1$, from Order $0$ to Order $1$ at
$\sigma = \ln 3/\ln(3/2)$, and from Order $1$ to Order $5$ at $\sigma = 1 + \ln(64/7)/\ln(81/64)$ and from Order $5$ to Order $41$ at $\sigma = 1 + \ln(m_5/m_{41})/\ln(\theta_{41}/\theta_5)$, between
$126$ and $127$ (the tops among the Orders up to $200$). Near such a crossing two terms of equal size can cancel.

\begin{observation}\label{obs:pzeros}
Newton's method from a grid in $1 \le \Rea s \le 14$, $0 \le \Ima s \le 40$ finds five zeros of $P$,
three near the first crossing and two near the second; the search may miss some. So the zeros of $P$
seem to lie in vertical strips around the crossings, which go on to the right as the upper record
Orders take over (their floors are the ones closest to $1$): the strips of the Newton polygon of the
points $(\lambda_n, \ln m_n)$, as for exponential polynomials.
\end{observation}

\subsection*{The primes}

The function $Z$ was built on the drop words alone, the atoms of the free monoid of
Section~\ref{sec:euler}. Looking back, the floors give its primes a second reading. Among the drop
words the word $10$, the cycle $1 \to 2 \to 1$, is the primest: its slope $F = \theta_1 = 3/4$ generates all the floors,
and the drop words of one Order differ only in where their common power of $F$ is centred. Write
$F^{\langle n\rangle}$ for $F^n$ moved into $[1/2, 1)$ by a power of $2$.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:primes}
Let $F = 3/4$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] $\theta_n = F^{\langle n\rangle} = F^n\, 2^{2n - p_2(n)}$, and so
  $P(s) = \sum_n m_n\, F^{n(s-1)}\, 2^{(2n - p_2(n))(s-1)}$.
\item[(b)] For a drop word $w$ of Order $n$ and length $L$, with centre $x^*_w = \rho_w/(2^L - 3^n)$,
  every $x$ of its class has $T^L(x) - x^*_w = F^{\langle n\rangle}(x - x^*_w)$.
\item[(c)] A word of Order $n \ge 1$ and length $p_2(n)$ with its ones at $t_1 < \dots < t_n$ is a drop
  word iff the slacks $d_i = p_2(i-1) - t_i$ are all $\ge 0$, and then, for every $x$ of its class,
  \[
  \frac{T^L(x)}{x} = F^{\langle n\rangle}\Bigl(1 + \frac{1}{6x}\sum_{i=1}^{n} \frac{1}{2^{d_i}\,F^{\langle i-1\rangle}}\Bigr).
  \]
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(a) $3^n/2^{p_2(n)} = (3/4)^n 2^{2n - p_2(n)}$, and it lies in $[1/2, 1)$. (b) $2^L T^L(x) = 3^n x + \rho_w$
and $2^L x^*_w = 3^n x^*_w + \rho_w$; subtract and divide by $2^L$. (c) Between two ones the level
falls, so the proper prefixes have level above $1$ iff the levels just before the ones do, and those
after the last one, the last of which, of length $L - 1$, has level $3^n/2^{L-1} > 1$. Just before
the $i$-th one the level is $3^{i-1}/2^{t_i - 1}$ (for $i = 1$ the empty prefix: $t_1 = 1$), and it
is above $1$ iff $t_i - 1 < (i-1)\alpha$, that is $t_i \le p_2(i-1)$. For the ratio,
$\rho_w = \sum_i 3^{n-i}2^{t_i - 1}$ (each one adds $1$, then is halved $L - t_i + 1$ times and tripled
$n - i$ times, all over $2^L$), so $\rho_w/3^n = \sum_i 2^{t_i-1}/3^i = \frac16\sum_i 2^{p_2(i-1) - d_i}/3^{i-1}
= \frac16 \sum_i 2^{-d_i}/\theta_{i-1}$, and $T^L(x)/x = \theta_n(1 + \rho_w/(3^n x))$.
\end{proof}

So $F^{\langle 0\rangle} = 1/2$ and $F^{\langle 1\rangle} = 3/4$ are the slopes of the two integer cycles
$0$ and $1$ of Section~\ref{sec:euler}, the Order $n$ contributes the one power $F^{\langle n\rangle}$
with multiplicity $N(n)$, and its drop words are that power taken about $N(n)$ centres, the cycle
points of B\"ohm and Sontacchi. Seen from the start $0$ instead of the centre, a drop ratio is
$F^{\langle n\rangle}$ times a factor made of the lower powers $F^{\langle 0\rangle}, \dots,
F^{\langle n-1\rangle}$, each divided by $2$ to the slack of the word at that one; the slack $0$
throughout (the ones as late as allowed) gives the largest factor at a given size of $x$; the
smallest starts differ, so the drop ratios there come in another order. For Order~$4$,
$F^{\langle 4\rangle} = 81/128$, and the three drop words $1111000$, $1110100$, $1101100$ have slacks
$(0,0,1,1)$, $(0,0,1,0)$, $(0,0,0,0)$; at their smallest starts $15$, $7$, $59$ the drop ratios are
$2/3$, $5/7$, $38/59$.

\subsection*{Side by side}

\medskip
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.27\linewidth}p{0.31\linewidth}p{0.33\linewidth}@{}}
\toprule
 & $\zeta$ & $Z$\\
\midrule
primes & the primes $p$, commuting & the drop words, a free monoid; the floors are the powers of $F = 3/4$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:primes})\\
M\"obius & $1/\zeta = \sum \mu(k)k^{-s}$ & $1/Z = 1 - P$: $-1$ on each drop word, $0$ beyond\\
Euler product & over $p$ & over the prime cycles of $D$, with the integer cycles $0$ and $1$\\
pole & $s = 1$, residue $1$ & $s = 1$, residue $1/\mu$ (Terras)\\
zeros & trivial ones and the critical strip & none ($1 - P$ entire)\\
on $\Rea s = 1$ & no zeros & no other pole (Theorem~\ref{thm:line})\\
near $\Rea s = 1$ & zero-free region in $\log t$ & $1 - \Rea s_k \asymp \|k\alpha\|^2$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:bend})\\
functional equation & $s \leftrightarrow 1 - s$ & none; no symmetry line\\
real parts & all $1/2$ (RH) & dense in $[z(1/2), 1]$ (Computation~\ref{comp:fall})\\
count to height $T$ & $\frac{T}{2\pi}\log\frac{T}{2\pi e}$ & $T\ln 2/(2\pi) + O(1)$, one per band\\
spacing & GUE statistics (conjectured) & deterministic: one curve $z$, rotation by $\alpha$\\
explicit formula & $\psi(x) = x - \sum_\rho x^\rho/\rho - \dots$ & Theorem~\ref{thm:explicit}\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The shape is that of the prime zeta function rather than of $\zeta$: an entire (here) or
logarithmically singular (there) $P$, and the poles of $1/(1 - P)$ where $\zeta$ has zeros.

%------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{The Lean formalization}
\label{sec:lean}
The library of this series~\cite{GitHub} holds the Lean~4 files of this paper, with Mathlib and without
\texttt{sorry}, in \texttt{AcceleratedCollatz/Zeta/}: the masses and counts there, the floors in
\texttt{Zeta/Floor/}, the best of the Order in \texttt{Zeta/Best/}.

\subsection*{The masses and the floors}
Theorem~\ref{thm:mass} is formalized in Lean~4 with Mathlib, without \texttt{sorry}, in the module
\lean{TCycleCount} of the library of this series~\cite{GitHub}: \lean{choose\_p2\_eq\_split} (the identity,
for every $n$, with \lean{admSet n} for $\Drop(n)$ and \lean{splitOrders n} for $S(n)$) and
\lean{mem\_splitOrders\_iff} (the test by powers). The proof is the one of Section~\ref{sec:mass}:
\lean{card\_wordSet\_eq\_sum\_tset} (Lemma~\ref{lem:bij}), \lean{tset\_of\_admissible},
\lean{tset\_append} and \lean{exists\_ladder\_split} (Lemma~\ref{lem:split}). The analytic results of
Sections~\ref{sec:zeta} to~\ref{sec:poles} and~\ref{sec:compare} are not formalized; the computations are scripts.

For the floors the library has the
record Orders as the semiconvergent denominators of $\alpha$ (paper~2~\cite{Paper2},
\lean{upperRecord\_iff\_semiconv}, \lean{lowerRecord\_iff\_semiconv}), and the identity of
Theorem~\ref{thm:counts} with its condition (\lean{choose\_p2\_eq\_split}, \lean{mem\_splitOrders\_iff}
in the module \lean{TCycleCount}). The module \lean{TFloorGaps} proves Theorem~\ref{thm:gaps},
in exact rationals and with no Farey sequence: the floors $\theta_n$ =
\lean{blockFactor}~$n$ obey the product rule $\theta_{x+y} \in \{\theta_x\theta_y, 2\theta_x\theta_y\}$
(\lean{blockFactor\_add}); the end ratio from $\theta_m$ to $\theta_{m+k}$ is $2\theta_k$, and to
$\theta_{m-k}$ it is $1/\theta_k$ (\lean{fwd\_add}, \lean{fwd\_add'}). With $\theta_a$ the lowest and $\theta_b$
the highest of $\theta_1, \dots, \theta_N$, the next floor above $\theta_m$ is $\theta_{m+a}$, $\theta_{m-b}$ or
$\theta_{m+a-b}$ in the three ranges of $m$, at the three end ratios (\lean{gap\_up}, \lean{gap\_down},
\lean{gap\_mid}), and $a + b \ge N + 1$ (\lean{add\_ge\_of\_extremes}); each case uses only the product
rule and the two extremes. The two extremes stay the same up to Order $a + b - 1$
(\lean{extremes\_until}; distinct Orders have distinct floors, \lean{blockFactor\_inj}), and $a + b$ is
the next record Order (\lean{next\_record}): $\theta_{a+b}$ is $\theta_a\theta_b < \theta_a$ or
$2\theta_a\theta_b > \theta_b$. Along the record walk the last records keep
$a\,p_2(b) - b\,(p_2(a) - 1) = 1$ (Remark~\ref{rem:unimod}; \lean{FloorWalk} in the module
\lean{TRowLength}), and with it \lean{rowLength\_eq\_min}: the largest number of parts
$m_1 + \dots + m_k = n$ with $p_2(m_1) + \dots + p_2(m_k) = p_2(n)$, the splits behind the sum of
Theorem~\ref{thm:counts}, is the least of $d\,p_2(n) - n\,(p_2(d) - 1)$ over $d \ge 1$.
Corollary~\ref{thm:stair} is not formalized; the computations are scripts.

\subsection*{The best of the Order}
The modules \texttt{AcceleratedCollatz/Zeta/Best/} (namespace \lean{AcceleratedCollatz.Best})
state and prove the results below in Lean~4 with Mathlib, without \texttt{sorry}. Its words (\lean{List Bool}, a one for an
odd step), the map $T$ (\lean{tStep}), the word of a start (\lean{tBits}), the constant $\rho$
(\lean{rho}), the Order (\lean{order}), admissibility (\lean{Admissible}) and $R(n)$ (\lean{rhoMax}) come
from the library of the series~\cite{Paper2}, restated in the terms of this paper in \texttt{Base.lean}. Only exact
statements are formalized; the table of bests (Appendix~\ref{app:table}) is script data, and so are
the two searches and the observations. Of that table Lean checks what a certificate line says
(Remark~\ref{rem:cert}): \lean{cert\_rows} proves it for each of the $378$ lines, the kernel doing the
arithmetic (no \texttt{native\_decide}); that no start below $W$ has a higher ratio stays with the searches. Table~\ref{tab:lean} lists the Lean names.

The width identity, on which the first runs of the scan rested (Remark~\ref{rem:width}; the rerun with the count does not), is
\lean{WidthIdentity} of that library; its range to Order $10\,781\,273$ uses a range check outside
Lean~\cite{Paper2}; the Orders of this paper lie within Terras's own range (Remark~\ref{rem:width}).
Lemma~\ref{lem:dinkelbach} is formalized for any finite family of ratios; that the steps end follows
from \lean{step\_lt}, as the family is finite. Böhm and Sontacchi's paper, with the cycle point of
Definition~\ref{def:cycle}, has a Lean formalization of its own~\cite{BohmSontacchiLean}, the first paper
formalized in the Collatz Conjecture Challenge~\cite{CCChallenge}.

\subsection*{How this was made}

The Lean files, the scripts and this text were written in conversation with Claude (Anthropic):
ideas were tried numerically first, in exact integers, then stated and proved in Lean, compiled
locally, and every open end was logged for the next session; the long runs (the scan, the tie
search) ran on the author's computer. Some of it went better than planned: the ties, first
searched word by word, turned out to need only the multiples of one number below the window
(Remark~\ref{rem:ties}). The author checked the statements and takes responsibility for them.
The same workflow is open to any reader: the Lean proofs can be checked on any computer, and the
scripts recompute the table of bests and its certificates.

\appendix
%------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Detectors that fail}
\label{app:failed}
One line each, with the reason (measured; scripts in \texttt{src/lean\_files/zeta/scripts/}).
\begin{itemize}\setlength{\itemsep}{0.2ex}
\item \emph{The scaled wall word as an ideal staircase:} every word starts and ends below it; the best's
  $2$ to $8$ crossings (Orders $4$ to $14$) are not special, and by the area between the two it ranks
  anywhere ($3$ to $1337$ at Orders $5$ to $13$) (\texttt{aim\_cross.py}, \texttt{aim\_area.py}).
\item \emph{Ideal lines and curves (diagonal, ruler, $\log$-shaped):} they measure area only; the best
  ranks anywhere (by the diagonal: $2$ to $6425$ at Orders $5$ to $13$) (\texttt{ideal\_lines.py}).
\item \emph{Trailing $1$-bits of the start:} no relation with the best.
\item \emph{Inverse trees:} the inverse tree of $91$ holds $21$ of the $106$ bests of Orders $1$ to $124$ in
  $(91, 2^{22})$, $20\%$, against $28\%$ of the odd starts in that range: no excess
  (\texttt{paper\_numbers.py}; \texttt{inv\_hub.py}, \texttt{inv\_grow.py}).
\item \emph{The backward branch and bound:} correct, but costs more nodes than forward (Order $22$:
  $96\,743$ primed against $1\,505$; \texttt{mitm\_search.py}, \texttt{boo.py}).
\item \emph{A guessed $\theta$ from the previous Order:} exact with halving, about twice the nodes of the
  seed $s_0$ (Orders $2$ to $75$: $8\,408\,907$ against $4\,317\,574$; \texttt{paper\_numbers\_slow.cpp}).
\item \emph{Candidates from earlier Orders} (one $1$ inserted per Order step, beams of $8$): beat $s_0$ at
  Orders $6$, $8$, $107$ only, of Orders $3$ to $124$ (\texttt{boo\_beam.py}).
\end{itemize}
